| Scope refusals per session | the third counted refusal of a session (`AGENT_SESSION_REFUSALS`: `out_of_scope` or `attack`; `needs_data` never counts, `other_library` only with `AGENT_OTHER_LIBRARY_COUNTS`, and a paste note whose `out_of_scope` went on is no refusal, capped instead at `AGENT_PASTE_NOTE_RESCUES` per user and day) closes it: that refusal and every later turn of the session answer `session_closed` ("reload the page to start a new session"), the later ones before the judge. The counted refusal before the closing one carries the fixed `close_warning` sentence (`AGENT_SESSION_CLOSE_WARNING`), after a live test of 2026-10-14 closed a session unwarned. Counted from the session's withheld messages, so the count ends with the session; a closed session's data is not carried into the next one (follow-up). The per-user strikes (`AGENT_ATTACK_STRIKES`, 3 `attack` verdicts per day across sessions and the dataset judge) end in the `locked_out` refusal for the day (the `budget` text before 2026-10-14, which read as a spent usage limit); the strikes live in the instance's memory and reset at midnight UTC or on a restart (a redeploy or a scale-to-zero) | the ScopeGuard, the `/messages` and `/dataset` routes |
| Service's day budget | once `AGENT_GLOBAL_DAILY_TOKEN_BUDGET` (100,000,000 weighted tokens, about $10, since 2026-11-01; 24,000,000 before) is spent, every admin and every user who still has their own budget gets the `service_paused` refusal (a paste or a conversion `429 service_paused`) until the usage book's new day at 00:00 UTC. Owner request of 2026-11-02: the text says the whole chat paused, not the user's own limit, and `GET /v1/status` answers `paused` and `resets_at`, so the chat frame's tab shows it as its mood (`resting until 01:00`) before the user types, read again while the tab is visible | `ledger.day_refusal`, `UsageBook.paused` |
| Daily budget | a user already over the daily token budget (`AGENT_DAILY_TOKEN_BUDGET`, 10,000,000 weighted tokens, about $1, since 2026-11-01; 8,000,000 before) gets the `budget` refusal at once, before the judge; a public user also has 50 pipeline runs a day (`AGENT_DAILY_PIPELINE_RUNS`, 40 before). An admin has no daily token budget, pipeline-run count, data-run count or message limit of their own since 2026-11-01 (owner decision: one fan-out booked 3,047,083 weighted tokens in that day's measurement), so the global budget is the admin's only daily cap on model spend; the strikes bind every tier, and pastes and conversions keep their per-user limits, while the fan-out's copies bind no admin (only the store's 256 MiB cap) | the `/messages` route, the Budget plugin (`UsageBook.daily_ok`) |
| Admin tier | `X-Anyplot-Tier: admin`, which the BFF sets from the identity its admin gate verified and only an allowed caller can send (the caller check answers first); anything else is the public tier, so a BFF without the header keeps today's limits. A header, not the `adm_` prefix of the user id, so the id stays opaque and the tier is one explicit assertion | `caller_tier` in `agents/main.py`, `agent_context` in the BFF |
| Anonymous public caller | since 2026-11-02 (launch package): the public BFF sends the page's random tab id as `X-Anyplot-Anon` and the client's network (the IPv4 address or the IPv6 /64) as `X-Anyplot-Network`; the service keys the user on the tab id salted with its per-process salt (`pub_` + 16 hex digits) and binds it for the UTC day to the salted network, so neither value is stored, logged or recomputable after the process ends. Such a caller is always public | `require_user` in `agents/main.py`, `ledger.anonymous_user`, `ledger.network_key` |
| Per network (per IP) | the anonymous users of one network share 4 sandbox runs queued or running (`AGENT_IP_MAX_RUNS`, `error{code:"run_limit"}`), 100 pipeline runs and 20,000,000 weighted tokens a UTC day (`AGENT_IP_DAILY_PIPELINE_RUNS`, `AGENT_IP_DAILY_TOKEN_BUDGET`, the `budget` refusal), and twice the per-user minute and day of pastes, file conversions, chat turns and outcome beacons (`IP_WINDOW_FACTOR`, the same `rate_limited` and `daily_limit` answers): twice the per-user caps, for shared networks. A network also holds at most 8 live sessions (`AGENT_IP_MAX_SESSIONS`, `429 session_limit`) and 32 MiB of the dataset store's 256 MiB (`AGENT_IP_DATASET_BYTES`; past it a paste or a data change is refused like a full store), so one machine cannot fill the memory or the store every user shares. A tab id costs nothing to mint, so the per-user caps alone bound a tab, not a machine; without the token cap one machine could spend the global budget and pause the chat for everyone. The address comes from Cloudflare's `CF-Connecting-IP`, which a caller who bypasses the edge can forge until the origin gate is armed | the run queue (`RunQueue.submit`), the usage book (`UsageBook.bind_network`, `own_tokens_left`, `runs_ok`, `_limit`), `create_session` and the dataset store (`Services.session_networks`, `DatasetStore.owner_of`) |
| Public lane's queue share | at most 10 waiting entries in the `normal` lane (`AGENT_PUBLIC_QUEUE_MAX_WAITING`; one more ends its turn with `error{code:"capacity"}`), of the queue's 60: every waiting entry is a chat stream that holds one of anyplot-api's 40 request slots | the run queue (`RunQueue.submit`) |
| Public streams per API instance | at most 20 public chat turns relayed at once per anyplot-api instance (`AGENT_PUBLIC_MAX_STREAMS`; one more is `503 capacity` before it reaches the agents service), so the catalogue keeps the other half of the instance's `--concurrency=40` | `open_public_message_stream` in `api/routers/agent_public.py` |
| Concurrent renders per instance | 1 (`AGENT_RENDER_CONCURRENCY`), one theme per slot, for every backend and every route; a waiting pipeline render gets a freed slot before a waiting theme toggle, first come first served whichever run-queue lane its run is in, and the `pipeline_render` attribution line reports the wait (`slot_wait_s`). It stays 1 with five runs in flight (owner decision of 2026-11-01): a second renderer instance would break the cancel path and the `503 busy` handling | `SerialRenderer` (`render/serial.py`), applied in `Services.backend` |
| Theme toggle | one render; it waits for a render slot at most `AGENT_REQUEST_DEADLINE_S` - `AGENT_RENDER_TIMEOUT_S` (120 s), then answers `503 capacity`; a theme that failed the host gates twice is answered from its record | `theme_render.py` |
| Dataset size | at most 1,048,576 bytes of paste (204,800 until 2026-10-14; computed data 2,097,152), 20,000 rows, 50 columns and 200 characters per cell; one past any of them is refused before any model call (`413 too_long` for the bytes, `422 unparseable` for the rest). The canonical `data.csv` can grow to about 3.8 times the input (`7e15` becomes `7000000000000000.0`); the parser refuses a parse whose canonical CSV passes `MAX_CANONICAL_BYTES` (4 MiB, the renderer's `data_csv` limit, 2 MiB before), so the 1 MB `stress-csv-expansion` case (3,954,696 bytes canonical) still fits. Measured on a 914,795-byte paste of 19,999 rows: the parse 0.5 s, the pre-filter over every cell 0.22 s, one dataset-judge part (the judge reads headers and a sample, never every row). The dataset store accounts each version at its canonical CSV (up to 4 MiB) and profile, and a session keeps its current version and five undo versions, about 25 MiB at worst, so its cap rose from 64 MiB to 256 MiB (about ten such sessions per instance, inside the service's 4 GiB) | the `/dataset` route and `parse.py` (stress cases `stress-cap-*`, `stress-over-*`, `stress-csv-expansion`) |
| Dataset profile in the adapter request | at most `MAX_ADAPTER_PROFILE_BYTES` (16 KiB of UTF-8, measured like the root's `get_dataset_profile` limit on the profile inside its `<user_data>` fence, so neither CJK or emoji cells nor tag text that the fence escapes can stretch it): the sample rows go first, then the top values past the five most frequent, then the rest of the top values, then the ranges and statistics, and every column stays. 47 text columns of long values make the full profile 26,911 characters (29,491 bytes; the model-facing JSON carries no null field) | `briefs.adapter_profile` in `render_adapt_request` (stress case `stress-cap-profile`) |
| Plotted points | scatter marks plus line vertices over `MAX_PLOTTED_POINTS` (500,000 since 2026-10-14, the owner's bound after the paste and computed-data caps rose: 20,000 rows of 24 series pass, the stress cases' 610,000 and 1,000,000 computed points still trip it; 200,000 before) are G9, an advisory DQ-03 line for the repair. Measured on WSL2: a Lorenz attractor of 1,000,000 steps renders in 2.0 s of CPU (2.1 s wall) at a 479 MiB peak and still renders with the address space cut to 610 MiB, so G9 is its bound. At 10,000,000 steps it peaks at 889 MiB and fails from 1,015 MiB down, so under the 1 GiB address space only a few MiB of platform overhead decide between G9 and R1. A 50,000-squared distance matrix fails at once under the 1 GiB address space (R1) | the harness probe and `gates.py` (stress cases `stress-compute-*`) |
| Probe texts | the probe measures the first 2,000 drawn texts (`PROBE_MEASURE_LIMIT`), because each extent costs CPU time under the sandbox's limit, and keeps at most 400 text boxes per render, the ones past a canvas edge first, so G3 sees every clipped text among them; `texts_total` counts every drawn text | `harness.py` |
| Missing glyphs | the probe lists at most 16 code points without a glyph (`GLYPH_SAMPLE_LIMIT`); G10 names at most three, each label at most 26 characters, and accepts a count of at most 0x110000, so its line fits the reviewer's 300-character note even for a forged probe | `harness.py` and `render/gates.py` |

The stress cases (`agents/evals/fixtures/cases/stress-*`) each name the bound that must stop them; `python -m agents.evals.matrix --cases stress --static` checks the model-free ones without a model, and the [fixtures README](../../agents/evals/fixtures/README.md#stress-cases-what-bounds-them) records what each measured.

A typical "Create plot" costs 4 LLM calls (root twice, adapter, reviewer); a repair adds one, and the review of a render repaired after a rejection one more; each free-text turn adds one judge call; the theme toggle costs none.

The run queue (`agents/anyplot/run_queue.py`) is an in-process FIFO in front of the sandbox runs, the memory, rate and cost limiter of the instance. Since 2026-10-14 a turn takes its entry mid-turn: the `/messages` route runs the ADK invocation in a task of its own and gives the request ledger `run_slot` (`TurnSlot.take` in `agents/main.py`), which `plot_pipeline` and `run_data_code` await before they render or run code; the queue positions it reports go through the stream's outbox while the tool is blocked. Since 2026-11-01 (owner decision) it runs five runs at once for the whole instance (`AGENT_RUN_CONCURRENCY`, 1 before), starts at most one every 10 seconds (`AGENT_RUNS_PER_MINUTE` 6, with the starts spaced 60 / 6 seconds apart; one a minute before) and holds 60 waiting entries: a 15-library fan-out had taken about 20 minutes through one slot and one start a minute, while each run renders for about 5 s of its 60 to 240 s. It has three lanes, which share that concurrency, rate and length and differ in their per-user cap and their order. `normal` is every user who is not an admin, today and after the public launch: two runs per user (`AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS`, 3 before). `admin` holds the admin's runs, the fan-out above all: five per admin (`AGENT_ADMIN_USER_MAX_RUNS`), first come, first served with the `normal` entries; the admin's other exemptions live in the usage book. A `premium` entry goes before every other one, under the `normal` cap; nothing sets `premium` yet, it is the lane for users who later pay for their own tokens. The `/messages` route picks the lane from the tier the BFF asserts (`X-Anyplot-Tier`), never from the client's `source: fanout`. The runs' model calls wait on the network, but every render takes the one render slot (`AGENT_RENDER_CONCURRENCY` stays 1), first come first served, and the `pipeline_render` line reports that wait as `slot_wait_s`; the 10-second gap between starts spreads a fan-out's renders. The gates run in a worker thread, so one run's PNG checks never stall the others' streams. While a run waits, the stream sends `status{step:"queued", position, waiting}` at once, on every change, and every 15 s unchanged, so idle proxies keep the stream open; `position` 1 runs next and `waiting` counts every queued entry, this one included. The BFF restarts its turn budget on each of them and on the first event after the wait, as before, because it never assumed the wait comes first. A client that disconnects while it waits leaves the queue, and `POST .../cancel` takes a waiting run out at once and ends the turn. A full queue or an expired wait fails the turn with `capacity` and aborts it, so the root spends no closing call and the page sees the same `error{code:"capacity"}` as before. Since 2026-10-21 (owner decision) a user's browser windows work in parallel, each in a session of its own, and only their sandbox runs share the queue: `submit` refuses a user's entry past their lane's cap (`AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS`, 2, or `AGENT_ADMIN_USER_MAX_RUNS`, 5, waiting and running together across sessions) with `QueueUserLimit`, before it looks at the queue's length, and the turn fails the same way with `run_limit`, so the page can say that the user's own runs in other windows fill it. `GET /v1/status` reports the caller's own cap as `user_max_runs`. The run registry, which answers `409 run_active`, covers every turn in flight and theme toggles per session, never per user, and its stale sweep frees an entry whose stream vanished after the maximum wait (while its sandbox run waits) or the deadline, plus a margin of 30 s. A turn counts as use of the session's dataset, so the idle sweep does not take it while the run waits. `GET /v1/status` reports `waiting` and `in_flight`. Runs that `adk web` starts bypass the queue, because they never pass through `/v1`; their renders still go through the one render slot.

The queue length is the owner's formula and assumes that a run ends within the 60 s rate window. A run that takes longer holds the only slot past the window, so every later start slips by the difference: with one run in flight, ten accepted entries and 90 s runs, the last four wait the full maximum and end with `capacity`. Admitting by an estimated start time (the position times the larger of 60 s per start and the recent run time) would keep that promise; it is an open decision.

### Two steps: data first, then the plot

Owner decision of 2026-10-11: "Use with my data" is an explicit two-step flow. Step 1 is the data, and it ends with the user's confirmation that the table and its bindings fit the plot. Step 2 is the plot, as described above. The owner's principle: in any real analysis the data must be right first, or everything after it is wrong.

Principles:

1. Step 1 is a stop only when it has to be. A clean paste (the parser happy, the bindings complete, no ambiguity warning) shows the preview and the bindings with `.create_plot()`, whose one click confirms them and starts the plot. Nobody with a clean CSV has to talk to an agent.
2. The model never writes data rows. It will choose operations from a fixed set, the server applies them with pandas to the stored canonical CSV, re-parses the result and stores it as the next dataset version with a change list and `.undo()`. The transcriber for free text is the one exception, and its table is confirmed by the user before it is stored.
3. One truth on the screen: the preview table is always the current version. Every model step shows what changed and needs the user's confirmation. Step 2 stays locked until the data is confirmed, on the server: the pipeline answers `not_ready` with `data_unconfirmed` (`load_inputs` in `agents/anyplot/pipeline.py`), so neither the model nor a stale page can start a plot on data the user has not looked at.
4. The most common real preparation is aggregation (single bookings into sums per month or category), so it belongs to the first set of operations.

Built since 2026-10-11 (the gate): `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/confirm` stores a fingerprint of the dataset id and the bindings as the user saw them (`anyplot.confirmed`, `confirmation_fingerprint`); the pipeline runs only while the fingerprint of the current state equals it. A new dataset gets a new id, so a paste or a transcription's `.use()` drops the confirmation by itself, and so does any binding change through the `PUT` route or the root's `set_bindings` tool, also one that lands between the confirmation's read and its write, because the stored fingerprint then names bindings the session no longer holds; a library switch keeps the data, the bindings and the confirmation. Since 2026-10-15 (live test round 2) there is one exception for the root's `set_bindings`: bindings that only leave out members of a confirmed number family (`y2`, `y3`, …), every other binding exactly as confirmed, keep the confirmation (`session_state.narrows` and `narrowed_confirmation`, `AGENT_NARROWED_BINDINGS=keep`; `reset` restores the old rule; owner decision of 2026-10-15: "e.g. drops the 2nd line"), so a step-2 note such as "only co2_per_capita, title …" plots in the same turn, and the tool result says `data_confirmed`. Leaving out a grouping, text or boolean role such as `series` (which merged two countries into one line in the review) or any single role, or renumbering a member so that `y1` plots another column, asks for a confirmation again, which the next `.create_plot()` click gives. A first plot's `change_request` that stops on `data_unconfirmed` or `incomplete_bindings`, or gets no queue slot, is kept in `anyplot.pending_request`, its `expected_texts` with it in `anyplot.pending_expected_texts`, and the bindings of that moment in `anyplot.pending_bindings` (`pipeline.keep_pending_request`, `hold_request`). A bare `.create_plot()` checks the render for the kept texts; a run that brings a request or texts of its own checks only its own, since the root never sees the kept ones and a withdrawn title must not count as a miss (`chosen_texts`); stored texts are read through the `PipelineArgs` validator (`kept_texts`). A note written for other data or other bindings never applies (owner decision of 2026-10-15). Any new data version (a paste, `prepare_data`, `generate_data`, `run_data_code`, `.to_long()`, undo) drops it at once. The bindings are judged at the run, not at each write (since 2026-11-05, after the UX walk of that day, where a user's unbind-and-rebind of the same column on the way dropped a kept "top 10"): the first-plot run takes the note while the bindings in force keep every binding of that moment, roles open then free to fill (the step `incomplete_bindings` waits for; `pipeline.pending_fate`, `session_state.fills_open_roles`), and drops it when the user rebound a column it was written for. The turn that keeps a note holds it for the bindings and the data it ends with (`_settle_kept_note` in `agents/main.py`), so the root's own `set_bindings` or `prepare_data` in that turn, before or after `keep_plot_request`, never voids it. The result tells the root `kept_request` (`used` or `dropped`) and the `bindings` it drew with, fenced as user data, and `root.md` has it describe the plot by those, never by an earlier turn's words. A change of an existing version neither takes nor clears the note: the next first-plot run joins it to its own request (`merged_request`, at most 600 characters, the older note cut with `…` rather than dropped), and it stays until a run ships a plot (`settle_pending_request`), so a failed run loses nothing; a failed run holds it for the bindings it drew with. A library switch keeps it. The session block tells the root whether the data is confirmed, and the root answers `data_unconfirmed` by asking the user to check the data and press `.create_plot()`, which it cannot do itself. The page shows the step in the status line at the chat frame's foot (`step 1 of 2 · …`; a step header `1 data · 2 plot` until 2026-11-06) and `.create_plot()`, whose click confirms the data and its bindings as shown and starts the run only when the confirmation went through (since 2026-11-04; a separate `.confirm()` before); the eval harness and the development fixture confirm on the user's behalf, because their data is the developer's own.

Since 2026-11-07 a plot button's turn plots exactly what its click confirmed. In two persona tests of 2026-11-06 the user pressed the plot button right after a data change and got "not confirmed yet": the data turn had derived a column the user wanted plotted (a points-per-game column in place of the points, a ratio as one more series) and left its binding to the user, and the root then bound it inside the `create_plot` turn. Adding a series is no narrowing, so the click's fingerprint no longer matched and the run stopped on `data_unconfirmed`. Two changes close it:

1. **The action turn only reads and plots.** The route marks the turns of `create_plot` and `carry_plot` (`RequestLedger.plot_action`; the fan-out's `.plot_all()` sends these in every library's session), and ToolSafety lets such a turn call only the reads (`get_dataset_profile`, `get_spec_brief`, `get_current_code`, `query_data`) and `plot_pipeline` (`PLOT_ACTION_TOOLS`, an allowlist, so a tool added later is refused there until it is listed). Every other tool, `set_bindings`, `prepare_data`, `generate_data`, `run_data_code`, `set_language`, `keep_plot_request`, `forget` and `switch_plot`, answers `plot_as_confirmed`, before its arguments are checked, and the root plots all the same. A route that runs `plot_pipeline` itself on an action turn was the alternative; it would have had to forge the tool call and its answer in the session's events and bypass the per-turn counters and the Budget plugin that the tool path already passes, for the same invariant one check gives.
2. **The data turn binds the column.** When the user asks for a new column in the plot, `root.md` has the root call `set_bindings` right after `prepare_data` in the same turn, with every binding in force plus the new column on the role the user means (the next free member of a number family for an extra series, or the role of the column it replaces). A required role that the turn's own data change left open, one that had a column before the change, is bound the same way when one column fits it; the root never tells the user to bind a column it can bind. A role that was open before the turn follows the conversion lines of the session block instead, so for a message that asks for another change the root binds nothing to it and makes no conversion or row number for it (a number column a conversion made still fills it through `carry_bindings`, as before): unscoped, the rule had the root add a `row_number` for an empty axis to "drop the Total row" in 7 of 8 prep-eval runs (2026-11-07). The server cannot make this binding itself: nothing in the operations says whether a derived column replaces a role's column or joins a family.

The same day closed a related misfire: after a re-paste with a note, the root answered with the rule for a table typed into the message ("I can't read data from the message itself"), although the table had arrived as that turn's attachment. The session block's line for a paste the root has not summarized (`agent.PASTED_LINE`) now says that the table came in as an attachment and is stored, and that the typed message is its note, and `root.md` scopes the typed-table rule to messages without that line.

Since 2026-11-08 code holds the turn's bounds that `root.md` used to state (prompt-modules plan v2, guards 2c to 2f):

1. **Caps per turn.** ToolSafety counts each tool's calls from the turn's allowed calls (`RequestLedger.calls_of`), so a refused call never counts, and refuses the call after the cap (see [Bounds](#bounds)) with its code and a fixed message: the turn has used up its calls of that tool, so the root answers with what the turn's results hold and says plainly what could not be done. The caps are checked once, after the allowlist, the plot action's guard and the arguments. The "never more than twice" and "once per turn" sentences left `root.md`; its "fix the call once" rules stay.
2. **No plot after the turn's own data change, or on the summary turn.** ToolSafety refuses `plot_pipeline` before the run when the turn stored a new data version (`prepare_data`, a rebuild from `from_version` without an operation, `generate_data` or `run_data_code`, each with an `ok` answer), or has one still under way, which a run in the same model answer would race (`data_changed_this_turn`), and on the summary turn of a paste while the data is unconfirmed (`summary_turn`). A summary turn on confirmed data still plots. The refusal leaves what the run's own `not_ready` would (`pipeline.stop_not_ready`): it keeps the first plot's request, writes the `pipeline_run` line with the `not_ready` reason and the session's last run, and answers with a fixed message, the reason (`reason`) and `request_kept`. The page gets no `plot` event and no failed plot card, and the turn's `done` brings the new data unconfirmed, which drops the page's confirmation as before. The turn's record keeps the refusal as such, with the outcome `kept` when it kept the request. `root.md` keeps the two sentences that ask for no such call (the summary turn never calls `plot_pipeline`; no plot in a turn whose `prepare_data` changed the data): they save the call. A plot after a `set_bindings` that voided the confirmation still runs into the run's own `not_ready` `data_unconfirmed`, with its `plot` event, since no data version changed.
3. **The plot buttons' line.** On the turns of `create_plot` and `carry_plot` (`RequestLedger.plot_action`, also in each session of the admin fan-out) the session block says that the turn is the user's action to create the plot from the data and the bindings as confirmed, and to call `plot_pipeline` with no arguments (`agent.PLOT_ACTION_LINE`). `root.md` lost that opening instruction; it keeps the rest of the rule, the refused tools and the recovery after `plot_as_confirmed`.

Built since 2026-10-11 (the operations): the root tool `prepare_data(operations)` with at most five operations per call, each a Pydantic model of the fixed set in `agents/anyplot/data/prepare.py`: `rename` (column, name), `drop_columns` (1 to 10 columns), `drop_rows` (`where` conditions on one column each, all of which must hold: `eq`, `ne`, `contains`, `is_missing`, `gt`, `lt`, at most three; a value is compared, never evaluated), `set_type` (number, datetime or text, with the date format and the decimal separator; to number is for the columns the profile still shows as text, because a column whose every cell carries the same unit is already numbers after the paste, the unit in its name such as `Anteil (%)`: one unit such as `%` or `EUR` is removed and the cells are read as the parser reads a number, the other mark a thousands separator between three-digit groups only, and when more cells read with the other decimal mark the whole column is read that way and the change line says so; since 2026-11-05 a pasted number column of whole numbers and `1.234` shapes that nothing else in the file settles carries a warning (`dots read as decimal points`, `decimal_points_ambiguous`) and keeps its cells as pasted on the paste's version (`StoredDataset.pasted_cells`, never sent to the page), because the canonical text has lost the trailing zeros (`7.000` is stored as `7`): `set_type` to number with decimal `,` reads that column again from those cells, `1.234` as 1234, while the rows are still the paste's (a drop, sort, group or reshape earlier in the call refuses it as `type_mismatch`, and a later version says to undo to the paste first); a cell that cannot be read becomes missing, more than 10 % of them is a `type_mismatch`; to text the column stays text through the re-parse of every later version, stored with the version as `text_columns`; `MM/DD/YYYY` on a column that is already dates swaps day and month only for a slash column the parser flagged as ambiguous), `aggregate` (by one or two columns, a value column, `sum`, `mean`, `count`, `min` or `max`, and a `month`, `quarter` or `year` period for a datetime key, written as text labels such as `2024-03`; since live test round 5 of 2026-10-15 also `hour`, `day` or `week`, which keep the key a datetime, the first moment of its bucket: a date for a day and for an ISO week (its Monday), a time with its offset for an hour, so "a daily mean per sensor" keeps a time axis; the bucket follows the column's own clock as stored, its single UTC offset or UTC for a column the parser converted from several offsets, and the change line names that offset), `sort`, `pivot_longer` (2 to 20 columns into a name/value pair; a long format over the 2 MB cap of computed data, `MAX_COMPUTED_BYTES`, is an `invalid_operation`; 200 KB until 2026-10-14) and `pivot_wider` (the distinct values of a column become at most 50 columns; duplicate pairs are refused, never aggregated silently), and, since 2026-10-12, `classify` (a new text column whose cells are labels: each row gets the label of the first of 1 to 10 rules whose `drop_rows`-style conditions all hold, else `otherwise` or a missing cell; labels are at most 40 characters, never a token the parser reads as a missing cell such as `NA` or `-`, exempt from ToolSafety's link rule like a rename's name, and the column stays text through every re-parse). `classify` came from the owner's case of 2026-10-12: a generated sine table on `bar-feature-importance` had no text column for the `feature` role, and "positive is class 1, negative class 2" had no operation, so the root had to refuse. Since 2026-10-14, `derive` adds a number column from number columns by one formula of a closed set: `ratio` (`a / b`) and `difference` (`a - b`) of two columns, `sum` of 2 to 10 columns in the same row, `share` of one column in the total of its group (`within` 0 to 2 key columns), and three formulas that walk each group in the order of `order`, a number or datetime column: `percent_change` from the previous row, `cumsum` (the running total) and `index` (the group's first row = 100; the last two by owner decision of 2026-10-14). A text order column is a `type_mismatch` with a hint to `set_type` it, because it would sort alphabetically (`April` before `Januar`, `10` before `9`); repeated order values within a group are refused (aggregate first), and a row without an order value keeps an empty cell; `percent` multiplies a ratio or a share by 100, and a division by zero or a missing input leaves the cell empty, which the change line counts. It came from the owner's live test of 2026-10-14: "which year has the largest share of foreigners?" on a long table (year, origin, count), where the root could only ask the user to compute the column; now it derives the share, drops the other rows, sorts, and answers from the new profile. Since 2026-10-15 no operation loses precision without its change line saying so (owner rule): `set_type number` reads whole numbers exactly (never through a float64, which rounded `9007199254740993`) and counts the cells it can store only rounded; a `sum` aggregate, a row `sum` or `difference` and a `cumsum` of whole-number columns add in Python integers (pandas' `Int64` sum had wrapped around past 2^63 without a word), and a result past the int64 range is a float64 whose rounded values the change line counts, a count the line's length cap never cuts; `pivot_longer` of whole numbers beside decimals counts the whole numbers past 2^53 that the shared float64 column rounds; a date-time keeps its fraction of a second and its UTC offset in `data.csv`; `set_type datetime` without a format reads a year-first cell (`2024-06-01T13:00:00Z`) year, month, day, chosen per cell (pandas with `dayfirst` had read it as 6 January), and reads a column with different UTC offsets (summer and winter time) in UTC with a change line that names the offsets, as the parser writes such a column (owner decision of 2026-10-15); times with and without an offset in one column are a `type_mismatch`, since no time gets a zone by guess. A condition (`drop_rows`, `classify`) on a column with an offset compares moments, its value converted to the column's offset; a value without an offset there, or with one on a column without, is refused with a message that says which to give (it had crashed the tool with a `TypeError`). A condition on a whole-number column compares exact integers (`9007199254740992` and `9007199254740993` are two ids, though one float64), a value with a fraction equal to no cell. A float result (a ratio, a share, a mean) counts its whole-number inputs past 2^53 in the change line, and an integer result that leaves int64 counts every value past 2^53 it writes as a float, also one a float holds exactly (2^63). Since 2026-10-15, `make_date` adds a date column built from whole-number part columns of the same row: a year (four digits), then a month (1 to 12) and optionally a day, or a quarter (1 to 4), or an ISO week (1 to 53), a part left out being its first (a year and a month give the first day of each month); a day without a month, a quarter or a week beside another part, a value out of its range and a row that is no calendar date (31 April) are refused, never moved; a text month column may hold month names and abbreviations in the eleven refusal languages (en, de, fr, es, it, pt, nl, pl, zh, vi, ru), compared without case and diacritics, an unknown name refusing it (owner decision of 2026-10-15); ISO week 1 can start in late December, which the change line says. `set_type` to datetime gained the formats `DD/MM/YYYY`, `YYYYMMDD` and `YYYYMM` (also on a number column), `YYYY-MM`, `MM/YYYY`, `MM.YYYY` and `YYYY`; the compact ones take exactly 8, 6 or 4 digits (`2024115` is refused, never guessed), `YYYY` on numbers says "read as years", and `MM.YYYY` on a column the parser read as numbers (`03.2020` as 3.202) pads four decimals only when every cell then is a month of a year 1000 to 2200, at most 300 years apart, so a column of ordinary decimals (`2.2`, `3.5`) is refused. Since live test round 7 of 2026-10-16 the parser itself reads a column of months (`2025-01`), years 1000 to 2200, as dates, each the first day of its month as `set_type` `YYYY-MM` writes it, with a warning that names the reading (`parse._as_periods`): read as text, a monthly series had put `GWh` on x. A span that only looks like a month stays text, because a date would change what the cells mean: seasons (`2009-10`, `2010-11`, each second part the next year's last two digits) and bins (`2000-05`, `2005-10`), and any column whose name says ids, codes or seasons (`Saison`, `Code`, `Nr`). Quarters (`2025-Q1`) stay text too: as a date they would lose the spelling that says quarter; `set_type` reads them on request. Since the live test of 2026-11-01 (the owner's RTX 4090 price table, `Nov 2026` to `Dec 2025` newest first, left `line-timeseries-rolling`'s date role open, and the root asked the user for a date column and then whether to reverse the rows), the parser also reads a month name with a four-digit year in one cell (`Nov 2026`, `November 2026`, `Okt. 2026`, `2026 Nov`, `März 2026`, `2026年3月`, in the eleven languages of `data/months.py`, `months.month_year`) as the first day of its month, with a warning that counts the cells; a two-digit year stays text (`Nov 26` is as often 26 November, and the century would be a guess), and so does a column that mixes forms or holds one cell of neither. `set_type` to datetime without a format reads the same cells, and a cell pandas would only fill in (no digit, such as a name `May`; a time alone, which it puts on today; no year, which it makes the year 1) is a failure instead of a made-up date. A minus written as the minus sign (`−`, U+2212) before a number is a sign for the parser and for `set_type number`, with a warning or a change line that counts the cells. A date column's profile says which way its dates run down the rows (`ColumnProfile.order`), and the root sorts a newest-first date column of the plot ascending in the summary turn, never asking. When the bindings leave a required role open, the server dry-runs `set_type` on every free text column in code (`data/conversions.py`, `prepare.dry_run`: dates for a role that takes dates, numbers for one that takes numbers, never numbers from a column of dates) and lists each candidate in the session block with its counts, `certain` (every cell converts, nothing guessed) or `uncertain` (some cells would be missing, or the reading guesses the day and month order, the century of a two-digit year, the decimal mark or thousands separators), and the operations: a `set_type` with `name`, which writes a new column (`Month (date)`, `Monat (Datum)` in the session language) beside the original, and a `sort` when its dates run newest first. The server makes every certain line itself on the summary turn of a paste (`agents/anyplot/paste_conversions.py`, since 2026-11-05), before the root's first model call. The root used to make them with its own `prepare_data` call, and in the prep eval it skipped the date column, or made it and dropped it again (`en-note-rename-year-column`, 3 of 3 runs). The root's before-agent callback (`agent.make_paste_conversions`) runs after the scope guard, so a refused note changes nothing, and after the note's language detection, which names the new columns. It applies the lines' operations through the same path as `prepare_data` (`prepare.apply_and_parse`), all into one data version of their own, so one `.undo()` takes back exactly these conversions. The session block then lists them under "Conversions the server made for open roles" with their counts and new column names (`Candidate.made`, the state record `anyplot.conversions_made`), and the summary names each as the root's own change; their change lines join the `done` event's `dataset.changes`. The root never makes, repeats, drops or renames such a column unasked. A note that asks to keep the source column as it is keeps it, since the original stays beside the new column. The root proposes an uncertain line as its one question, an offer still waits for the user's request, and a later turn, also after an undo, never makes a line by itself. A request in words to read the column of a listed `set_type` line as numbers or dates ("read Anteil as numbers") is that `set_type` on the column in place, without the line's `name` and with its `sort` on that column: the new column beside the original is only for a conversion nobody asked for. Only an `uncertain` line whose reading guesses the day and month order, the century or the decimal mark still gets its question first, and a date column's offer stays a `date_number`. A column read in place from another type is its own source in `Prepared.converted`, so it fills an open required role that takes it exactly as a named conversion does, and never a role a binding holds; it also keeps its other roles, since no second column stands beside it. Before that rule, the root copied the line's operations for such a request in most runs of the prep eval's `de-percent-not-count` and `en-percent-unit-mixed`. The service binds the new column to the role (`carry_bindings`: a new date column as before, a converted number column through `Prepared.converted`), the user confirms the data as usual, and `.undo()` takes it back. A column whose name or cells say ids, codes or spans of years is no candidate, and a whole-number column is one only for a date role and only with a year header (`temporal.year_score` 2), because prices of 1149 to 3000 look like years. An en dash is never a minus, so `10–20` and `–5` stay text. The converted column takes its source off the optional roles, since `Month` on `series` would split the plot into one series per row. The defaults fill the roles in spec order, so one number column sits on `x` and `y` stays open (`bindings.axis_slot`): dates for the axis then move that column to `y`. When no column and no conversion can fill the role, the last fallback is the row number (`bindings.row_slot`, `row_number` in the operations): on a required axis it counts as `certain`, the column `index` holds 1 to n, and the root says so in one line ("x: row number 1 to 6, because the table has no x column"). A role that names each row (`category`, `label`, `name`) gets it only as a question: a number says nothing as a label, and 8 of the 21 specs with such a role and one measure are distributions by group, where it would make one group per row. A date, a measure or a series role never takes it. A row-number column the paste already has is reused, and `/dataset/copy` runs none of this: it copies confirmed data byte for byte. Since 2026-11-07 the candidate lines stay out of the session block for the rest of a turn once the root's own `prepare_data` changed the data in it (`ledger.prep_ops`, `agent.session_context`): recomputed on the new version, the row-number line came back worded as `certain` on the next call, and the root added a `row_number` nobody asked for to a chain of a drop, an `aggregate` and a sort in about half the prep-eval runs. The lines of the conversions the server made stay in every call, since the summary names them, and the next turn lists the candidates again. The text labels of an `aggregate` by `month` or `quarter`, and the names a `pivot_longer` of month headers writes, stay text through the re-parse. A role that labels (`categorical`, `text`) takes a date column, so the months of a heatmap or a grouped bar chart keep their role; by default only a required role takes one, only up to 60 distinct dates (a column of months past them too, `ColumnProfile.monthly`, as it bound when it was text, and the plot writes it as `Nov 2026`, never as an ISO date), and after a real text category (`ts, host, latency` keeps `host` as the bars; 20,000 timestamps are never a stacked bar's components). Dates are read with Python's parser and stored at microseconds, so years outside pandas' nanosecond range (1677 to 2262) no longer crash the tool. A date column whose every day is at most 12 keeps its day and month order open from version to version (a warning carried by `with_date_notes`), and `set_type` swaps it in either direction: `MM/DD/YYYY` on a column read day first, `DD/MM/YYYY` or `DD.MM.YYYY` on one read month first. All this came from live test round 3 and its review: a MeteoSwiss table of `Year` and `Month` had no date for `line-timeseries-rolling`, the root tried `run_data_code`, which cannot read the user's table, and then asked the user to add the column by hand; the root prompt now names the operation and forbids handing data work back when an operation can do it. A date column the change made (a datetime column the version before did not have) fills an open required date role in `carry_bindings` (owner decision of 2026-10-15: code, not the model). No free code (an injection surface), no joins, no model-guessed fills: the operations only move, type, aggregate, drop, label, empty or compute from cells that are there (since 2026-11-07 `compute` takes an expression, checked by the whitelist of `generate_data` before a cell is read, see "Built since 2026-11-07 (derived columns)" below), and `ToolSafety` validates the list against the same models before the tool runs. The server applies them with pandas to the stored canonical CSV, re-parses the result with `parse_dataset` (the dataset judge does not run again: the only new text is a rename's name or a `classify` label, which the model writes from a user message the scope judge already checked) and stores it as the next dataset version (`agents/anyplot/dataset_versions.py`): the previous version joins `anyplot.dataset_history`, which keeps the last five (older ones leave the store; the idle sweep takes a session's history only together with its current version), `anyplot.dataset_version` counts up from the paste's 1, the bindings are carried over (`carry_bindings`: the ones still valid stay, the roles left without one get the defaults), and the confirmation drops by itself because its fingerprint names the dataset id. The tool answers the change lines (one English line per operation with its counts, fenced as `<tool_notes>`) and the new profile (fenced as `<user_data>`), or a `PrepareError` code (`unknown_column`, `type_mismatch`, `empty_result`, `too_wide`, `invalid_operation`) with a message that names columns at most, or since 2026-11-07 `unsupported_operation` with a fixed sentence (see "Built since 2026-11-07 (derived columns)" below). The page learns about a changed dataset from the `done` event: after a turn that changed the dataset id it carries `dataset: {preview, profile, bindings, warnings, roles, changes, version, context_lines}`, the dataset route's shape plus the turn's change lines (`roles` since the formulas below), and `data_confirmed: false`; the BFF relays a `done` of up to 512 KiB whole (every other event stops at 64 KiB), which the largest dataset the parser accepts fits (`agents/stream.py`, `MAX_DONE_EVENT_CHARS`). `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/undo` (the user's `.undo()` button; the model has no undo) restores the newest history entry as the current dataset, bindings carried over, unconfirmed again, and answers like the dataset route (`422 nothing_to_undo` for a paste that was never changed). The step-1 summary is the action `{"action": "summarize_data"}` on the messages route (judge skipped, like `create_plot`; since 2026-10-14 the turn also carries the notes pasted with the table, already judged at the paste, after a preamble and fenced as `<user_notes>`, and `get_dataset_profile` returns them under `user_notes` for later turns, with a fixed session-block line saying they exist, so the root uses them as context for the whole chat and acts on a clear request in them at once, the plot itself still waiting for the user's confirmation): the root reads the profile, which now carries `mean` and `median` for numeric columns, and answers in two to four sentences (rows, columns with types and units, missing values, values that look like percentages, the proposed bindings, and since 2026-11-01 a question only when the data would be read wrong without the answer, such as dates whose day and month order reads both ways), never claiming the data is right: the user confirms. The live eval `agents/evals/prep.py` scores the root's choices on `agents/evals/fixtures/prep/` by the table the turn leaves, compared with the table the expected operations make from the same version through the same server code (since 2026-11-08; before, by exact operation match, which it still reports as `ops_exact`), with an 80 % gate (see [Run the prep eval](../../agents/README.md#run-the-prep-eval)). The page asks for the summary right after every parse that stores a dataset (a paste, or a transcription's `.use()`; never after an undo or a library switch), so it is the first turn in the thread, and the composer takes data requests in step 1. A `done` with `dataset` replaces the preview, the profile and the bindings, takes the page back to step 1, and shows a `# changed` block above the preview with the change lines and `.undo()`; after an undo the block names the restored version and keeps `.undo()` while the version is above 1, until the service answers `nothing_to_undo`.

Built since 2026-11-04 (dates as numbers): the owner tested a table of 1,000 customers (an `Index`, names, cities, phones and a `Subscription Date`) on `scatter-basic`, whose `x` and `y` both take numbers. `x` got `Index`, no column fitted `y`, and "convert subscription date to integer" had no operation: `set_type` to number reads text cells, `derive` takes number columns, and `make_date` goes the other way. `date_number` (`column`, `unit`, `name`) adds a number column beside the date column, which stays as it is:

- `year`: the calendar year.
- `days`: the whole days since the column's earliest date, which is 0. Calendar dates count, never the time of day.
- `timestamp`: the Unix time in seconds, in UTC. A moment with a UTC offset is converted, a date without a time counts from its midnight UTC, and a time without an offset is read as UTC; the change line says which. Whole seconds stay whole numbers for every year from 1 to 9999; a fraction of a second is kept as decimals, and the change line counts a value more than 272 years from 1970, where a float64 no longer holds every microsecond.
- `decimal_year`: the year plus the part of it gone by (1 April 2021 is about 2021.25; a leap year has 366 days). It reads well on a scatter axis and costs a few lines.

The year, the days and the decimal year follow the column's clock as stored, as the `aggregate` buckets do. A missing date stays missing, and the change line counts the dates converted and the cells left empty. Without `name` the column is named after its source and the unit in the session language (`Subscription Date (days)`, `Abodatum (Tage)`, `prepare.DATE_UNIT_WORDS`), so the root leaves the name out and the result is the same in every run. Any other column is a `type_mismatch` whose message tells the root to `set_type` it to datetime first, in the same call. The new column fills an open required role that takes numbers (`Prepared.converted`, `carry_bindings`), as a `set_type` with `name` does, and `.undo()` takes it back. Nothing converts by itself, because the unit is a choice: when a required role that takes numbers only is open and no other line fills it, the session block lists a free date column as an offer (`conversions.DateNumbers`), never `certain` and never a question. The root names the offer in one statement, with the four units, and makes it only on request; a request without a unit ("as an integer", "als Zahl") takes `days`, whose change line names the first date. An offer never takes the place of a conversion of a text column or of the row-number fallback.

Built since 2026-11-04 (text operations and range pairs): the agent case triage found a `range-interval` table whose ranges stood in one text column (such as `10–25`). The root could not turn the column into numbers and sent the user to a spreadsheet three times, and the default bindings had put a count on `min_value` and an average on `max_value`, so every row's minimum lay above its maximum. Four text operations close the first gap, a closed set whose texts are always literal, never a pattern:

- `split` (`column`, `separator`, `names`: 2 to 10 new columns) cuts each cell at the separator, any literal text such as `-`, `–`, `,`, `;`, `/` or ` to `. A separator of spaces cuts at every run of whitespace, and a `-` or `−` that starts a part right before a digit is that part's minus sign, so `-5-10` gives -5 and 10. A cell without the separator stays whole in the first new column, and the last one keeps the rest of a cell with more separators; the change line counts both.
- `extract` (`column`, `keep`, `name`) keeps the one number of each cell (`#12345` gives 12345, `CHF 1'200` gives 1200; a cell with none or with two, such as `10–20`, stays empty, and more than 10 % of such cells are a `type_mismatch`), only its ASCII digits, or only its letters with the spaces between them.
- `strip` (`column`, `prefix`, `suffix`, `name`) takes a literal text off the start or the end of each cell that has it.
- `replace` (`column`, `old`, `new`, `name`) writes `new` in place of every `old`; an empty `new` removes it.

Each reads the source column's canonical cell texts, whatever its type, and writes new columns appended last; the source stays as it is, so `.undo()` and the user's own check work as for every other operation. A new column is typed as `set_type` reads one (`prepare._typed`): numbers with the dataset's decimal mark, its thousands rules and at most 10 % of failing cells, which become missing and are counted, else dates, else text, which stays text through the re-parse. A number counts only in a cell the parser reads as one (`10 kg`, never `Q1`), leading zeros keep a column text, and a column with a cell of digits alone is never read as dates. The operations chain within one call: a `strip` of `#`, a `split` of the new column at `-` and an `extract` of a part's digits. `ToolSafety` exempts the literals (`separator`, `prefix`, `suffix`, `old`, `new`) and a split's `names` from the link rule, as it exempts a condition's value: they are compared with the cells or written into them, never followed.

The second gap is closed in the binder (`agents/anyplot/data/bounds.py`). A lower and upper bound pair is two single number roles of a spec whose names differ in one word, `lower`/`upper`, `low`/`high`, `min`/`max` or `lcl`/`ucl` (`min_value` and `max_value`, `ci_lower` and `ci_upper`, `low` and `high`); `error_lower` with `error_upper` (magnitudes) and a tornado chart's `low_value` with `high_value` (outcomes of two scenarios) are none. The default bindings never bind a pair whose lower column starts above the largest value of the upper one: both roles stay open, so the conversion check for open roles runs. A pair bound so by hand is the user's choice (owner decision of 2026-11-04: a person may bind high and low the wrong way round on purpose): it stays valid, nothing blocks the confirmation, every change of the data and every undo carries it over unchanged (`carry_bindings` leaves out only an inverted binding it would add itself), and it carries one neutral warning line that asks for nothing (telemetry code `bound_pair_inverted`); the root prompt mentions it once and then follows the user. For an open pair, the check offers a text column of ranges as a `certain` conversion (`conversions.RangeSplit`) when every cell is two numbers around one dash, read with nothing guessed, and the first is never above the second; ISO dates, ids such as `A-12`, spans of years, a cell of another shape and a unit leave the column to the user's request. Its `split` names the new columns after the column with `min` and `max` in the session language (`Range (min)`, `Spanne (Min)`), and `carry_bindings` binds the first to the lower role and the second to the upper one, whatever order the spec lists them in. Only two neighbouring parts of one `split` fill a pair together (`Prepared.splits`; two `extract`s of one column never do), and only when the first neither starts nor ends above the second (`bounds.ordered`), so `22-18` cells leave the pair open rather than swap it.

Built since 2026-11-08 (row fidelity, the data side): the owner decided that the plot draws every row and that a change of the rows goes only through the data step, where `prepare_data` counts it and `.undo()` takes it back. In the persona round of 2026-11-06, two plots changed the rows in their code while the confirmed data still held every one: a line chart of 27 measurements over 9 lengths came out as 9 averaged points, and a weekly series lost a week. Two deterministic facts, read from the profile and the bindings, make such changes visible for all 15 libraries (`agents/anyplot/data/fidelity.py`):

- **A key that repeats.** `KEY_ROLES` lists each spec whose plot type draws one value per key: a bar, slice or stage per category, a cell per pair, a point per x or date of each series. Spec families where repeats are the point (scatter, box, violin, strip, swarm, histogram, density) are never listed, and neither is a line that can turn back on itself, such as a parametric curve. The listed roles are marked `DataRole.key`. The key of a mark is the bound key roles plus every other bound role that takes labels or dates, since a series or a group splits the marks. When the bindings are complete and more rows draw a mark than the key columns can make keys (the product of their distinct values), rows share a key, and the binding check adds one warning with the counts, for example `x: column 'Length' holds 9 values in 27 rows; the plot draws one value per x, so rows with the same x need an aggregate first` (telemetry code `repeated_key`). A row draws a mark only when it holds the key, every required single measure (a candle needs all four prices) and at least the fullest member of a required family. The bindings route and `set_bindings` carry the warning among the binding warnings; the session block gives it a line of its own, `- Repeated key: x has 9 values in 27 rows, ...`, with the warning fenced below it, since it is a fact of the rows and not a column that misfits its role. The root prompt offers the counted `aggregate` of `prepare_data` and never sends a wish for one value per key as a `change_request`. The warning refuses nothing and changes no default binding, and a binding carried to new data keeps its column (`BindingCheck.fit_warnings`).
- **Rows without a value to draw.** A row whose bound column for a mark is empty draws no mark there. The pipeline puts one server line into the shipped version's `changes`, right after the loader's notes, for example `The plot draws no mark where a value is missing: 2 rows without y ('Exam Score')`. It counts the required roles that place a mark (a key, or a role that takes numbers or dates), never a computed or optional role, a text label, a column filled in one row at most, or a role that `SEPARATE_ROLES` names because its spec keeps a shorter list or a single value beside the rows, such as the event dates of an annotated line. Like the loader's notes, it never becomes a residual note, so the status stays the render's.

Built since 2026-11-09 (row fidelity, the prompt side): the shared adapter rule of all four languages (`adapter.md`, "The data contract") no longer lets the plot code group, pivot or filter the rows at will. The marks of the bound roles draw every row of `df`:

- **What the plot code may do.** It sorts, positions, sizes the axes, computes a computed role, and draws the statistic its plot type makes from the rows, such as a box's quartiles, a histogram's bins, a density, a fit, or a count or total per cell or tile that the spec describes. It splits the rows into series, groups or panels, each row in one of them, because a mask that partitions the rows draws every one.
- **What it never does.** It never leaves rows out by a value, a range or a threshold, keeps only the first, last, largest or smallest ones, samples or thins them, drops duplicates, or combines the rows of one key into one value where the spec draws a value per row or per key. The "Other" group of the palette rule is the one merge it makes. When rows share such a key all the same, it draws each of them, and the root has the repeated-key warning to explain it. The random draws that each language allows stay for jitter only: thinning above a cap would be a change of the rows without counts, so the four language slots and the validators' repair lines (`ADAPTATION_TARGETS["rng"]`) no longer name subsampling.
- **Missing values.** The plot code drops a row with an empty cell only for the mark that needs the cell, so the empty start of a rolling window never cuts the raw rows. It never names those rows in `changes`, because the server's line counts them.
- **A request that changes the rows.** A change request, an earlier request or a standing decision of the plot memory that asks for only some rows, the first or largest ones, a sample, no duplicates or one value per key is left out of the code, with one `changes` note that names it as a change of the data. A request about how the rows are shown, such as an axis range, a log scale, the order of the bars or a highlight, stays the plot's.
- **The root.** In the plot result, the root names the server's "The plot draws no mark where a value is missing" line in every reply to that result, as what the plot draws, never as rows removed, and never offers to fill the values in. For a left-out change of the rows, it offers the data step in plain words and sends such a wish to `prepare_data`, never as a `change_request`. Both notices are on the list that brevity never drops.

The rule is a prompt rule; no validator checks it yet. Where the rows reach the plot with a repeated key, it did not hold on its own: in the measurement of 2026-11-09, the adapter still averaged such raw rows per key in both runs (an ECharts bar chart and a ggplot2 heatmap), as it does on main. So the server guards that case in code (owner decision of 2026-11-09, `agents/anyplot/repeated_keys.py`):

- **A data step before the run.** When the confirmed data repeats a key of a spec that draws one value per key, `run_pipeline` first makes the `aggregate` that the repeated-key warning offers, through `prepare_data`'s own code. It aggregates by the key's columns and keeps every other bound column, storing the result as the session's next data version. The version has a number and the standard aggregate change line on the data card, `.undo()` brings the raw rows back, and the user's confirmation moves to it with the same bindings. The run then draws every row of that version.
- **The announcement.** The result's first `changes` line counts the step, for example `Rows that share a key were combined in data version 2: averaged 'Reading' over 27 rows into 9, one per 'Length'`. It is also the one change of a run that shipped nothing, and the result says `data_changed: true` either way (`PlotResult.combined`). The root names it in every reply to that result, as a change of the data, with the undo.
- **Which function.** `mean`, since a keyed spec draws a measurement per key, except for the specs whose marks add up, which `sum` (`fidelity.SUM_SPECS`: parts of a whole such as pie, donut, treemap and stacked bars, counts such as a Pareto chart or a funnel, and a waterfall's changes). A count role bound to a column of counts is summed too; the `count` function never fits, because it counts rows and drops the bound column.
- **When nothing is combined.** The server combines nothing for a spec whose measures need several functions (`fidelity.UNREDUCED_SPECS`: candles, OHLC bars and other price charts, a measure beside its bounds, goal or total, and an estimate beside its interval or spread, whose averaged bounds would bound nothing), for a spec with a bound role whose list lies beside the rows, for a key of more than two columns, and when a bound column besides the key holds no numbers. The binding warning stands then, and the root offers the step.
- **Not doubled.** A data step made before, by the user or by an earlier run, leaves no repeated key, so the run combines nothing more.

A static rule for the Python profile and a closed "left out" code that the server turns into the root's offer are open follow-ups. The eval matrix follows the rule too: the 45 `n5000` cases of `bar-grouped`, `heatmap-basic` and `pie-basic` hold raw records that repeat their keys, so they take the root's offered `aggregate` as a data step before the plot (`before_operations` in `case.json`, see `agents/evals/fixtures/README.md`), stored by the server's own code with no model call.

Built since 2026-11-07 (derived columns): in the persona tests of 2026-11-06, five of ten personas got no plot and two lost their second plot, because `prepare_data` could not derive the column they needed. It had no product, no power, no log, no change from the previous row, no moving average, no way to empty a missing-value code, and `aggregate` took one value column. The root then said the change had to come from the user's file. The owner decided on one package of operations with the rules of the data layer unchanged: exact precision, every change announced with its counts, one flat CSV, and no sandboxed pandas code, which would break the counts.

- `compute` (`name`, `expression`, `inputs`) adds a number column from an expression over number columns. `inputs` maps each short name of the expression to its column (`{"l": "Länge l in m", "T": "Periode T in s"}` for `4*pi**2*l/T**2`), since a column name is no name an expression can hold. The expression language is `generate_data`'s, checked by its AST whitelist before a cell is read (`generate.parse_expression`), so one language serves both tools: numbers, the inputs, `pi`, `e`, `tau`, `+ - * / ** % //`, parentheses and its functions. No comparison, condition or other call parses, and every input must appear in the expression. Where every input is a whole-number column and every step keeps whole numbers whole (`+ - * // %`, a power with a whole exponent, `abs`, `sign`, `min`, `max`), each row is computed in Python integers, exactly. Plain arithmetic (`+ - * /` over the inputs and number literals) is computed in exact decimals with one rounding to a float at the end, as `diff` and `rolling` compute, so `19.99 * 3` is 59.97 and never 59.96999999999999. Anything else (a power, a function, a constant) is computed in float64, as `derive` computes it, never rounded to `generate_data`'s eight significant digits. A cell stays empty where an input is missing, a step divides by zero, or a step leaves a function's domain or the number range, also when a later step would make the value finite again; the change line quotes the checked expression and counts the empty cells by reason.
- `diff` (`name`, `column`, `order`, `within`, `lag`) adds each row's value minus the one `lag` rows before it (1 by default) within its group, in the order of `order`, as the ordered `derive` formulas walk. `rolling` (`name`, `column`, `how`, `window`, `order`, `within`) adds the `mean` or the `sum` over `window` rows: a row and the ones before it. The first `lag` or `window - 1` rows of each group stay empty and are counted, as is a row whose window holds a missing value. Both work in exact decimals, so whole numbers stay exact and `12.3 - 10.1` is `2.2`, as an `aggregate` sum adds its cells. An order column may also hold the period labels an `aggregate` writes (`2024-03`, `2024-Q1`, `2024`), whose text sorts in time order, so "the last reading of each month, then the monthly consumption" is two operations in one call. A window over a span of time (seven days of irregular readings) is no option: an `aggregate` by `day` first makes it a window of rows, and the gap is named as `time_window`.
- `blank` (`columns`, `where`) empties the cells of one to ten columns in place where `drop_rows`-style conditions hold, such as the missing-value codes `-0.99` and `-1`. The rows stay, each column counts its emptied cells, and a column that would lose every value is refused. It empties and never fills: `prepare_data` still never invents or fills in a value.
- `keep_rows` (`n`) keeps the first `n` rows in their current order and drops the rest, counted ("kept the first 10 of 487 rows, 477 dropped"). After a `sort` it is the top N (owner decision of 2026-11-07): "the top 10 by revenue" is a `compute`, a `sort` and a `keep_rows` in one call. A table of `n` rows or fewer keeps every row, and the line says so. A question about the top rows still takes `query_data` with a `sort` and its `limit`.
- `aggregate` takes `columns`, two to ten value columns reduced with one `how`, and the reductions `first` and `last`: a group's first and last value that is there, in the order of the rows or, with a `period` on a date key, of its moments. The last meter reading of each month is `aggregate` by the date with `period` `month` and `how` `last`.
- A condition takes `values`, an "any of" list for `eq` and `contains`, in every operation with conditions. `classify` puts several values or keywords into one label with it (`Miete` or `Strom` is `Wohnen`). Its change line counts the rows left to `otherwise` or without a label, and names their three largest groups by the text column the rules test most often, so a fallback that took a category of its own is seen: a persona's `Sonstiges` had taken two categories without a word.

When no operation and no chain of them fits, the root calls `prepare_data` with the operation `{"op": "unsupported", "family": ...}`, where the family is one of `fill_values`, `edit_cells`, `add_rows`, `combine_tables`, `text_pattern`, `time_window` and `other`. The call changes nothing. The tool answers `unsupported_operation` with the `family` and a fixed sentence in the session language (`prompts/refusals.yaml`, `unsupported_<family>`, eleven languages), which names what the chat cannot do and never sends the user to a spreadsheet or their file; the root sends it word for word. The turn's `chat_turn` line counts the gap by its family (`capability_gap`, see [Usage telemetry](../reference/agent-telemetry.md)), never with a word of the request. The prep eval has eleven new cases for these operations (`compute` four times, `diff`, `rolling`, `blank`, the last value of two columns per month, two values into one class, the top 3 by revenue with `keep_rows`, and a fill request named as a gap), and its Fahrenheit case now expects a `compute` too. It compares a `compute` by the values it computes on invented rows, whatever short names and form the expression has.

Built since 2026-10-15 (questions): a question never changes the data. In the live test of 2026-10-15 the root answered "which country had the highest per-capita emissions in 2023?" correctly, but by filtering the user's dataset to the rows of 2023 with `prepare_data`, which stores a version, and then asked the user to `.undo()` it. The root tool `query_data(operations, columns, limit)` (`agents/anyplot/data/query.py`) applies zero to five operations of the same closed set as `prepare_data` (no code, only a `compute` expression its whitelist checks) to a copy of the current version and returns the first `limit` rows (1 to 25, default 10) in at most 8 columns, fenced as `<user_data>`, with the result's row count, a `truncated` flag and the change lines; the result is cut by rows to its 16 KB cap. Nothing is stored: the dataset, its version, the bindings and the confirmation stay as they are. The root prompt says that `prepare_data` is only for a change the user asked for, and that a question (a maximum in a year, a share per year, a trend) goes to `query_data`; a computed column the user then wants in the data is a `prepare_data` with the same operations.

Built since 2026-11-07 (earlier versions): the root reads and rebuilds from the original table or an earlier version, and says so (owner decision of 2026-11-06). In the persona tests of 2026-11-06 the chat told users to press `.undo()` or paste again three times, because both data tools read only the current version: categories after an aggregate, the pace per run after a weekly sum, and "all 20 teams" after a top 10. `prepare_data` and `query_data` now take `from_version`: `current` (the default), `original` (the first version of the current data: the paste, the opened file, the copy or a table computed in a session without data) or a version number the session block lists. A rebuild applies its operations to that version, or takes it as it was when there are none, and stores the result as the next version on top: the history is never rewritten, the user confirms the new data as after any change, and `.undo()` stays the user's button, one step back. The first change line says where it started ("from your original table: kept the first 20 of 487 rows, 467 dropped"), and the data card lists it with the others. The session block lists the versions the store keeps (`dataset_versions.version_lines`): the original's shape, then each later version's change lines and shape, at most the original, the five versions of the undo history and the current one, each line cut at 240 characters, fenced as `<tool_notes>` since the change lines name the user's columns. The original stays in the store after the history cap pushed it out of the undo history (`anyplot.dataset_original`), so a session holds at most seven versions; a new paste deletes it with the rest. A number that is gone is refused with a closed code: below the number of the current data's original it was a version of data a paste replaced (`other_paste`), from it up a version that was undone, left the history or was never given (`version_not_kept`). A plot button's turn still refuses `prepare_data` (`plot_as_confirmed`) and still allows `query_data`, from any version. The prep eval's cases can change the data before the message (`before_operations`) and score the version the turn starts from (`expected_from_version`).

Built since 2026-11-08 (one numbering): the session numbers every data version once, by owner decision of 2026-11-07. Until then the page counted the versions itself while the server's number started over with every paste and went back on `.undo()`, so since `from_version` a user who said "go back to v2" could get another table than the card called v2. Now every version the session stores takes the next number (`anyplot.dataset_top` holds the highest given, the stored version keeps its own as `StoredDataset.number`), a paste, an opened file, a copy or a computed table as much as a change, and no number is given twice: a paste, a change, an undo and a change are 1, 2, 1 and 3, a second paste then 4. An undo makes the restored version current under its own number, and the card says so (`data v1 · back to v1`). The card's label, the session block and `from_version` read the same number. Whether `.undo()` can go is the answer's `can_undo` (the history holds a version), never the number; `original_version`, the number of the current data's first version, lets the page count how far an undo went back for the `agent_data_undo` bucket.

Built since 2026-10-11 (formulas): owner decision of 2026-10-11, a function such as "sin x von -pi bis pi" is a deterministic data source in step 1, not a path around the data layer. The root tool `generate_data(variables, series)` takes a spec, never rows: one or two variables, each a range `{name, start, stop, points}` (`start` and `stop` are expressions over numbers and the constants `pi`, `e` and `tau`; 200 points by default, at most 5,000; two variables make a grid of every pair, the first variable slowest, at most 10,000 rows), and one to ten series `{name, expression}`. The server computes the table with NumPy (`agents/anyplot/data/generate.py`). The expressions are a whitelist over Python's `ast`, evaluated by walking the checked tree and never passed to `eval`: numbers, the variables, the three constants, unary `+` and `-`, `+ - * / ** % //`, parentheses, and the functions `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `asin`, `acos`, `atan`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `exp`, `log`, `log10`, `log2`, `sqrt`, `abs`, `floor`, `ceil`, `round` and `sign` with one argument and `atan2`, `min` and `max` with two, at most 200 characters and 100 nodes per expression. Anything else (attributes, indexing, strings, comparisons, conditionals, lambdas, comprehensions, keyword arguments, `^`, `2x`, a `#` comment) is `invalid_expression` with a message that names the construct, such as "'^' is not a power; write x**2"; the typographic operators `−`, `×` and `·` read as `-` and `*`, so the change line quotes nothing but the checked expression. Every value is a float64 array under `np.errstate(all="ignore")`, so an overflow, a division by zero or a logarithm of a negative number becomes a missing cell, never an exception; a change line counts such cells per series (`y: 12 of 200 values are undefined and left empty`), and a series with no defined value is `undefined`. The other codes are `invalid_range` (an endpoint that is not finite, equal endpoints, or a range so narrow for its points that two of them round to the same value), `too_many_points` (a grid over 10,000 rows) and `too_large` (a table over the 2 MB cap of computed data, `MAX_COMPUTED_BYTES`, which no grid within the point limits reaches with the generator's cells since the cap rose from 200 KB on 2026-10-14; the message names about how many rows fit, and for a grid how many points each). Every value is rounded to eight significant digits and written as the parser writes a number, the variables before the series are computed, so every row holds `y = f(x)` for the `x` it shows; the table re-parses as number columns and becomes an ordinary dataset version: the next version through `push_version` when the session has data (undo restores the previous one), or the first version of new data the way the dataset route stores a paste when it has none; either way the confirmation drops by itself and the bindings are the defaults for the new columns, never the ones made for the old data, even where a column name such as `x` returns (`push_version(rebind=True)`). The tool answers like `prepare_data` (the change line, such as `generated 200 rows: x from -3.1416 to 3.1416, y = sin(x)`, fenced as `<tool_notes>`, and the new profile fenced as `<user_data>`), and `ToolSafety` validates the spec against the same model first, with the link rule over every string. The plot and its export (`plot.py` with `data.csv`) use the generated table like any other. Since a first dataset can now come from a chat turn, `done.dataset` carries the spec's `roles` too, at most 50, like the dataset route. The dataset route refuses pasted text that looks like a formula with `422 formula` before the transcriber would read it, because a model would compute the rows (`looks_like_formula`); only text within the chat's 2,000-character cap is refused this way, because the page sends the refused text to the chat as a turn, and longer text reaches the transcriber, whose rubric computes nothing. The guard reads no delimited table, and reads the rest line by line and sentence by sentence, each of at most 300 characters: a formula is a call whose argument uses a variable (`sin(x)`, `sin x`, `√x`; never `Max(3)`, a name with a count), an assignment of an expression to a short name (`y = x**2`, `f(x) = 2x + 1`; never `Äpfel = 3x`), or a range phrase with numbers or `pi` next to a constant, a one-letter variable before it (`für n von 0 bis 10`), a power or a curve's name such as "Parabel" or "Sinuskurve". A unit is no sign of a formula (`m²`, `CO2 in t`), and neither is a word that data has as well, such as "curve", "Funktion", "exp" or "Quadratmeter". Text that holds its own values is data, whatever else it says: three numbers or more outside the formula's ranges, expressions, parameters and point counts (`Umsatz von 2020 bis 2024: 10 12 15`) send it to the transcriber. A formula request in one sentence of more than 300 characters is not caught; the transcriber's rubric covers it (compute nothing, write only the values the text holds). The scope judge treats a request to draw a function, curve or distribution shape as in scope, never `needs_data`, and four formula cases joined the prep eval.

Built since 2026-10-14 (data code): owner decision of 2026-10-14, a user may paste or type a snippet that generates the data (Python, JavaScript, R, a formula from a textbook, anything), and it must work offline. The root translates any non-Python input into Python and calls `run_data_code(code, source_language)`; `generate_data` stays until the evals show the new tool covers it. The server checks and runs the code in these steps (`agents/anyplot/tools/session.py`):
