a role the plot can compute (`DataRole.computed`: a spec bullet that starts with "Computed", "Calculated" or "Derived", such as `rolling_avg` of `line-timeseries-rolling`, or a role `COMPUTED_ROLES` in `core/spec_roles.py` lists for its spec: a value with a fixed formula over the spec's other roles, such as the Bollinger bands, the SMAs, EMAs and Ichimoku lines of a price table, the MACD signal line and histogram, or `ctl`, `atl` and `tsb` of `line-training-load-pmc`, and never a model fit or a value that needs an input no role carries; the list lives in code because only the spec pipeline writes spec files, in `core/` so that the pipeline's adaptability checker reads it too and asks no column constant for such a role (checker version 2), and a test checks each listed role against its spec and audits the role wording of every spec, so a role worded as a formula fails until it is listed or named as data with the reason) is optional, "both allowed" (owner decision of 2026-10-15): the defaults bind it only to a column whose name clearly holds it (its own name or every word of an alias such as `SMA 20`, `MA(20)`, `BBU_20_2.0`, `Upper Band`, `Fitness (CTL)`, `Chronic Load`, `7-day average`; never a bare word that names something else, such as `Upper`, `Fitness` or `Form`, and never a column whose name says it is something else, such as `Upper Shadow`, `Volume SMA`, `Fitness Age`, `Fatigue (RPE)` or a MACD), after the required roles have picked (a column a computed role could take ranks last for them, so `close` or `tss` keeps the one number column) and never a weak measure; on a tie the column that names the spec's window wins (20 for the Bollinger roles, 42 and 7 for CTL and ATL, and for the EMAs either pair the spec names, 12 and 26 before 50 and 200), and a bound column of another window (`SMA 200`) is a binding warning that the root names in its summary; the user may bind any fitting column by hand; left open it is never missing, the page's dropdown shows `— computed by the plot —` as its empty choice, and the spec brief tells the adapter per role whether to use the bound column or compute the value from the bound data, with the spec's own words for its formula and window, building an open role on its bound companions (the bands around a bound middle band) rather than independently, and naming the window an open role without bound companions takes (the long EMA's 26, or 200 beside a bound `EMA 50`); a name-only role (`DataRole.name_only`, listed per spec in `agents/anyplot/data/computed.py`: the MACD line of `indicator-macd`, the RSI of `indicator-rsi`, and the sample means, ranges, limits and center line of `spc-xbar-r`, plus the optional `close` and subgroup size their spec changes add) binds by the same strict name match only (`MACD_12_26_9`, `RSI 14`, `X-bar`, `UCL`, also a column with one value on every row, as limits are) but stays required: a price table leaves `macd` missing instead of drawing `Open` as the MACD line, and `Sample, X1, ..., X5` leaves the five summary roles missing instead of binding the raw measurements, and no conversion, row number or date offer fills such a role unless the column's name holds it; roles without typed bullets stay empty and the UI asks; a role that needs values of 0 or more never takes a column with a negative value, see `agents/anyplot/data/signs.py`: role names that are magnitudes in every spec, such as `radius`, `size`, `count`, `std`, `weight`, and `value` only in part-of-whole, area and flow plots such as pie, donut, treemap, sunburst, icicle, waffle, marimekko, sankey, rose and the basic funnel, never in signed ones such as waterfall or diverging bars; a binding of one by hand stays valid and becomes a warning in the session block, the `set_bindings` result, the PUT route's `warnings` and at the page's dropdown, through the role's `non_negative` flag). `bindings.apply(session, bindings, source)` validates roles against the snapshot's data roles and columns against the profile (since 2026-11-04 one column may serve several roles, such as the y and the size of a bubble or the value and the colour of a bar, without a warning, when the user binds it so in the data panel or the root does on request; only the members of one variadic family take a column once each, and the defaults and the conversions still bind each column once); the PUT route (through `session_service.append_event` with a `state_delta`) and the `set_bindings` tool both call it, and both answer `409` while a run is active.

Since live test round 5 of 2026-10-15 the parse also handles these cases:

- **Several tables in one paste.** A report sheet often holds two tables one below the other: a title, a revenue table, a blank row, a caption and a staff table. A table of its own starts after a blank line (or at the top) with a header (two or more distinct names, no value among them except years) under at most a few one-field caption lines, followed by at least two rows of its width with a value each (`_one_table`, `_table_starts`). A candidate with the same width as the table above needs evidence that it is a new header: a caption line above it, a name it shares with the header above (`Filiale` heading both tables), or text over a column that holds values above and below it (`Produkt,Menge` under a `Region,Umsatz` table, which joined would turn `Umsatz` into text without a word). So a blank row inside one table followed by a row of year-like numbers (`Bern,1500,1850,2040`) or a row of text in a table of text never splits it, and an ambiguous row of text over numbers splits with a warning rather than joining silently. The years of a wide header run one way (`2020,2021` or `2025,2024`). A one-field label above a row of text inside one table reads as a caption and splits (said in the warning). With two or more such tables the first is read; the lines before the first table's blank-line group (a title, an export's metadata) become notes without ever being read as a table's rows, the table's own caption stays its note, one warning names the tables left out, and the dataset route answers `tables` (one label per table: its header names and its row count, at most 10) and `table`. The page keeps the stored paste's text with its dataset and offers the others in a table picker beside the preview, which parses that text again with `table` (`POST .../dataset {text, table}`), as the sheet picker reads another sheet of a workbook. A single table with a blank line inside it, an export's metadata of one row above the table (Open-Meteo), a key-value block (`Station:`) and notes below the table stay one table, as before. Not handled: an R export with an empty first header followed by a second table is not split, because a header needs every name filled.
- **Row names.** A paste's own first column in one tool's exact shape leaves the table with a warning that counts its cells: `""` (R's `write.csv`) or `...1` (readr) over `1` to `n`, or `Unnamed: 0` (pandas) over `0` to `n - 1`, in row order. It runs on a paste and on a converted file's text, not on a confirmed transcription. Any other first column stays: row names that are names (car models), positions after a filter, an empty header over `0` to `n - 1` (pandas' `to_csv` itself, or a step column), and a column named `X` (R's `read.csv`) or `index`. Owner decision of 2026-10-15: such a kept column of row numbers (`bindings.row_number_columns`: a row-number name over `1..n` or `0..n-1`, each once) takes no default binding by its name, no family member and no optional role while the other columns can fill the required roles (`X, height, weight` binds x to `height`). When the required roles need it all the same (`X, weight` for x and y), the bindings are those of an ordinary table, so it keeps its natural x slot rather than transposing the plot.
- **A header in two lines.** A spreadsheet or an expanded web table with grouped columns writes its header in two lines of the table's width, such as `Location,Density,,Population` over `,/km2,/mi2,`. When neither line holds a value, the first shows a group (an empty cell, or one name over two columns) and the second its parts (an empty cell, a cell that repeats the one above, or a part repeated under each group), the parse joins them into one name per column, `Density /km2`, and says so in a warning. A browser's plain-text copy loses the spans (`Location, Density, Population, Land area, Ref.` over `/km2, /mi2, km2, mi2`, rows of 7 fields), so the text cannot say which names cover two columns: that paste is refused with the cause, never joined by guess. A header shorter than rows that all agree is named as the cause; the hint about quotes stays beside it only when every row has one field more, which a separator inside one value of each row could also explain.
- **Missing tokens in every column.** A token such as `NA`, `-` or `n/a` read as missing is counted per column in number and date columns too, not only in text columns, so the user hears about the four `NA` in `body_mass_g` as about the four in `sex`.
- **Whole numbers without `.0`.** A whole number in a number column is written in one canonical form, without `.0` (`87000` beside `120500.5`, as the Excel conversion writes it, where it was `87000.0` before; `2.0` as `2`), by the parser (`number_text`) and by every `prepare_data` operation. Owner decision of 2026-10-15: `30` and `30.0` are the same number, so this needs no note; only a change of value is said. A value a float64 holds only rounded, and any whole number past 2^53, keep the `repr` with its `.0`, and the existing rounding notes count them.

Since 2026-11-07 the parse also reads three shapes it had read wrong, the first two from the persona test of 2026-11-06:

- **Comment lines above a table.** A data file from R, numpy, gnuplot or a weather service opens with comment lines that start with `#`, `%` or `//`. When at least two such lines open the paste, the parse reads the table below them and keeps the comments as notes; a comment line that holds no letter or digit (a rule, a bare `#`) is counted with the left-out lines but carries no text. When the table below has no header line, its first line being a row, the last comment line names the columns, or the last two when the header wraps over them: in a table aligned with spaces each name sits under the column whose character range it overlaps most, so `decimal` over `date` becomes one column `decimal date` and `of days` stays one name, and in delimited text the line splits at the delimiter. A warning says where the names came from. A line names the columns only with two names or more, no value other than a header's years, and names for at least half of the columns; a column without a name becomes `column_N`. Comments that lead to no table, or to a table without a header that no comment line names, leave the paste to be read as before. One comment line alone is no comment block, because `#,Player,Goals` or `% change;Land` is the header of a table with a column named so; a marker right before a tab, a comma or a letter (`#\tName`, `#tbt`) is a cell too.
- **A statistics export with metadata, flags and `:`.** A spreadsheet download writes key-value metadata (`Unit of measure,Number`) above a header of years (`TIME,2019,,2020,`). Years read as values, so the header used to become a data row and the metadata the header. A first row that is a header of periods (a name, then two or more distinct years or months of a year running one way, among names and empty cells) is now the header when the next row is none and the line above names fewer cells. A key-value line never joins a header in two lines: an empty cell under an empty cell is no part of a group. A `:` cell, the mark for a value that is not available, is missing in a column that then reads as numbers or dates and is counted in that column's missing-cell warning; a text column keeps it. A column with an empty header right of a named number column whose cells are each one to three letters (`p`, `e`, `b`, never a code of two or three capitals such as `CH`) is a column of flags: it stays in the table as text, named after its number column (`2019 flag`), with one warning that counts the flagged cells, since a flag says something about its value that a plot may show. Thousands dots read by the existing rules once the `:` cells are missing. The export's legend below the table (`:,not available`, `p,provisional`) is padded to the table's width like every other line, so it read as rows without values and turned a year column into text: a record whose value cells are missing now extends the table only up to a blank line, and directly under the last data row it stays a row as before. The legend after the blank line is kept as notes below the table.
- **A Markdown table.** Chat assistants write a row of dashes under a table's header (`|---|:---:|`), which the parse used to join into the names (`Month -------`) or read as a row. In pipe-delimited text, a record whose every filled cell is such a rule (`---`, `:---`, `---:`, `:---:`) is left out as layout when it stands directly under the header: the record above has its field count, holds a name, and is the first record of that count since the last blank line. A row of dashes further down the table stays a row, since it may stand for missing values.

### Adapt and validate

`agents/anyplot/code/` has no ADK import. Every version has two forms: the **working form** contains the literal placeholder `df = load_user_data()` and is what the adapter edits; the **run form** has the loader substituted and the normaliser applied and is what runs and what the user downloads. Order of operations: apply the edits to the working form, validate with the ADAPTED profile (the placeholder is allowed), normalise, substitute the loader, then render and export the run form. `base="previous"` and every repair attempt always get the latest validated working form; if the previous attempt failed validation, the next one gets the original plus the previous attempt's edits as `previous_plan`.

- **Normaliser** (per runtime; for Python, every library with a save rule in `regions.SAVE_RULES`: matplotlib and seaborn, plotnine and altair since 2026-10-25, pygal and lets-plot since 2026-10-26): strip the `sys.path` guard, the catalogue header and the catalogue title (the title string becomes `""`; the call that shows it stays), and rewrite a save target that is a path ending in the theme file to the literal `f"plot-{THEME}.png"` (the second file of altair, pygal and lets-plot to `f"plot-{THEME}.html"`). Since 2026-10-26 the guard strip takes four more shapes in every library: an `importlib.util.find_spec` test around imports, a `try` whose `finally` only restores `sys.path`, `del sys.modules[...]` in the guard, and `importlib.import_module("m").ATTR`, which becomes `from m import ATTR`. Two libraries get rewrites of their own:
  - pygal's HTML write (`with open(f"plot-{THEME}.html", "wb") as f: f.write(chart.render())`) becomes `chart.render_to_file(f"plot-{THEME}.html")`, which writes the same bytes. Its other HTML and SVG writes (hand-built pages, post-processed SVG) are dropped, together with the statements that only fed them, also from a `with open(...)` body that assigns names nothing else reads.
  - lets-plot's star import becomes an explicit import of the names the file uses (from the static `lets_plot.__all__` of 4.11.0 in `letsplot_names.py`), the `export_ggsave` alias becomes `ggsave`, every save gets `path="."`, and the epilogue that moved the files out of `./lets-plot-images` is removed.

  The canvas rewrite is per library (`SaveRule.canvas`), and the canvas is never an LLM repair target:
  - matplotlib and seaborn: the normaliser drops `bbox_inches`, keeps the figure size and rescales the dpi so the output hits 3200x1800 or 2400x2400 exactly (within the canvas gate's 16 px when the aspect is a hair off and no dpi is exact).
  - pygal (since 2026-10-26): a chart built at another literal size with the target's aspect, in the catalogue 4800x2700 or 3600x3600, saves through `cairosvg.svg2png(bytestring=chart.render(), write_to=f"plot-{THEME}.png", output_width=3200, output_height=1800)` (or 2400 by 2400), with the same layout. A hand-built SVG passed to `svg2png` gets the output size from its root `<svg>`, and a size set after the chart's constructor leaves the save alone with a note. The rewrite applies to 94 catalogue files.
  - lets-plot (since 2026-10-26): only `scale=` on the PNG save changes, because the PNG is the `ggsize` times the scale. The rewrite applies to 117 catalogue files. Both rewrites exist because R2's pixel cap (11.52 MP) refuses a 4800x2700 or 3600x3600 PNG outright; lets-plot's scale even sits in the protected save, out of the adapter's reach.
  - plotnine and altair have no canvas rewrite yet. plotnine sizes its canvas on the save (`width=`, `height=`, `units=`, `dpi=`) and altair through the chart's size times `scale_factor`, padded by its PIL block, so for those two the normaliser only notes that the render relies on the R3 canvas gate, and the readiness scan reports the note as a `canvas:` reason.
- **Readiness scan** on the normalised original: `blocked` (the 13 map specs, a library that is not enabled, a SECURITY validator failure, no `THEME` block, a save call of the library's rule that writes anything but `f"plot-{THEME}.png"` (altair, pygal and lets-plot also `f"plot-{THEME}.html"`) or, for pygal, a file type its save name does not write, a lets-plot `ggsave` without `path="."`, or a last save of the PNG that is missing or not a module-level statement), `coupled` (literal limits, literal color-scale limits, centers and colorbar ticks, a colorbar at literal figure coordinates (`cbar_pos`), data-coordinate annotations, literal statistics, short palettes, hard-coded date locators; these findings go to the adapter as hints. The two colorbar categories since live test round 8 of 2026-10-16, where heatmap-basic in seaborn kept `vmin=0, vmax=100, center=50` for data of 32 to 93 and the top-left colorbar after the adapter dropped the dendrogram), or `clean`.
- **Edits** apply in order. Each `find` must match exactly once and may not overlap a protected region. Matching goes from strict to tolerant and is never fuzzy: an exact substring first, then the same with the blanks before line breaks ignored, then whole lines whose words agree once indentation and runs of blanks outside string literals are ignored, with the replacement moved by the one indentation shift between them. A string literal's text must agree exactly, and a line inside a multi-line literal never matches this way. The first tier that finds anything decides, so more than one match there is a failure. A `find` that matches, in any tier, only the code before an earlier edit of the same plan changed it is reported as such (`stale_match`). Protected regions are found by AST: the `THEME` assignment, every theme token (a module-level assignment that tests `THEME == ...` and is built only from literals, `THEME` and earlier tokens: `PAGE_BG`, `ELEVATED_BG`, `INK*`, `GRID`, `BRAND`), the placeholder line, and the final save statement: the last module-level statement that writes `f"plot-{THEME}.png"` with a save call of the library's rule (`savefig`, plotnine's `save` or `ggsave`, altair's `save`, pygal's `render_to_png` or `cairosvg.svg2png`, lets-plot's `ggsave`), because altair's, pygal's and lets-plot's files end on the HTML save, and altair's pad the PNG inside an `if` after the save that is protected. A theme conditional that reads the data, such as box-basic's `_tight_text_color = INK if THEME == "dark" else IMPRINT_PALETTE[tightest_idx]`, is plot code the adapter may change. After a plan, a protected statement must keep its meaning, compared as a syntax tree, so a full file that re-states a token in other quotes or spacing passes. A plan refused for that drift alone gets its protected statements restored once the round's check retry is spent (see the loop changes above). The `IMPRINT` palette list is not protected: it stays one literal list, assigned once, that keeps its existing entries in order and may only be extended with further positions from `core/palette.py`, which the validator checks. With more than eight groups the adapter keeps palette colors for the seven largest and draws the rest in the theme's muted ink as one "Other" group, which the reviewer accepts under VQ-07.
- **Loader**: exactly one `df = load_user_data()` in the working form. The server replaces it with `pd.read_csv("data.csv", dtype=..., parse_dates=[...])` using a relative path, so the exported code also works in notebooks. A number column needs no float option: since pandas 3.1 `read_csv` has one exact converter, so the plot draws the values `data.csv` holds (`float_precision`, which the loader set from 2026-10-15, is deprecated there and goes in a later version, which would break every exported file). A dataset with a date column adds `date_format="ISO8601"`: `data.csv` writes ISO dates only, and without it pandas guessed a format per column and warned "Could not infer format" for years such as 0001.
- **Validator profiles.** The SECURITY rules run on the normalised original (eligibility) and on every adapted working form: `ast.parse` on at most 48 KB; imports only from COMMON (numpy, pandas, math, statistics, datetime, collections, itertools, functools, textwrap, colorsys, re, and since the owner's decision of 2026-10-10 the pure computing modules warnings, typing, calendar, decimal, fractions, zoneinfo, enum, dataclasses, operator, bisect, heapq, numbers and cmath; not `string`, `locale` or `random`) or the per-library allowlist (the plotting submodules, for matplotlib and seaborn also the bundled `mpl_toolkits.{mplot3d,axes_grid1,axisartist}`, `scipy.{stats,interpolate,signal,cluster.hierarchy,spatial,optimize,special,integrate,ndimage,fft,linalg,constants}`, sklearn without `datasets`, `statsmodels.{api,nonparametric,tsa,graphics}`; since 2026-10-25 for plotnine that matplotlib stack plus `plotnine` and `mizani`, and for altair `altair` and the module `PIL.Image`, which the catalogue's padding block uses; `vl_convert` stays out, because altair reaches it internally and its own functions read files and open sockets for image hrefs; since 2026-10-26 for pygal `pygal` and `cairosvg`, and for lets-plot `lets_plot`, while `cairocffi`, `cssselect2`, `tinycss2`, `xml.etree` and PIL stay out of both; third-party helpers such as cartopy or adjustText stay out); never `statsmodels.formula`, `scipy.io`, `scipy.datasets`, `plotnine.data`, `plotnine.watermark`, `plotnine.animation`, `altair.datasets`, altair's `jupyter`, `utils.execeval`, `utils.server` and `utils.html`, pygal's `etree`, cairosvg's `test_api`, lets-plot's `geo_data`, `tilesets`, `settings_utils`, `frontend_context` and `plot.geom_livemap_`, sys, pathlib, importlib, subprocess, socket, urllib, requests, shutil, ctypes, pickle or io; `os` only as `getenv("ANYPLOT_THEME", ...)`; a `banned-import` message names the module and what to use instead, and the finding carries a label of the path for the attribution log (its longest prefix in a closed vocabulary of module names, else `?`); each admitted module was walked for names that read, write, fetch or evaluate, and those are banned (`warnings.formatwarning`, `showwarning` and `warn_explicit`, `zoneinfo.reset_tzpath`, `available_timezones` and `ZoneInfo.from_file`, `typing.get_type_hints` and `ForwardRef`, `operator.attrgetter` and `methodcaller`, `enum.global_enum`, calendar's `LocaleTextCalendar`, `LocaleHTMLCalendar` and `different_locale`, which set the process locale, and `calendar.main`, which reaches them and is banned on the calendar module only, because a column may be called `main`); module re-exports that reach what the import rules keep out are banned as attributes on any object and as `from` imports (`sys`, `os`, `builtins`, enum's `bltns`, `modules`, `inspect`, `subprocess`, `importlib`, `pathlib`, `shutil`, `socket`, `linecache`, `CodeType`, `FunctionType`), and so is any private name (one leading underscore) of an imported module, such as `collections._sys` or `dataclasses._FuncBuilder`; both rules cover the components of an import path below its root as well, so `import scipy.fft._backend as backend` is refused; a module bound by `import` is only ever the receiver of an attribute, never a value, so `[calendar][0]` is refused; and the dangerous private helpers a walk of the allowed modules found (`_sys`, `_locale`, `_random`, `_tzpath`, `_common`, `_getcategory`, `_FuncBuilder` and numpy's `_datasource`) are banned by name on any object, so a module reached through an attribute chain or a `from` import cannot hand them out either; banned names `eval`, `exec`, `compile`, `open`, `__import__`, `input`, `breakpoint`, `globals`, `locals`, `vars`, `getattr`, `setattr`, `delattr`, `memoryview`, `exit`, `quit` and the three-argument `type()`; every name, attribute or keyword matching `^__\w+__$` and any string literal containing a dunder identifier; banned attributes `.format`, `.format_map`, pandas `.eval` and `.query`, `memmap`, `ctypeslib`, `f2py`, `matplotlib.use`, `show`; banned calls: pandas `read_*`, `ExcelFile`, `HDFStore` and the I/O writers (`to_csv`, `to_pickle`, `to_parquet`, `to_excel`, `to_sql`, `to_hdf`, `to_feather`, `to_json`, `to_html`, `to_clipboard`, but not `to_datetime` or `to_numpy`), `np.load*`, `save*`, `savetxt`, `fromfile`, `fromregex`, `DataSource`, `loadtxt`, `genfromtxt`, `ndarray.tofile`, `plt.imread`, `matplotlib.image.imread`, `matplotlib.cbook.get_sample_data`, `mpl.rc_file`, `load_dataset`, `fetch_*`, `urlopen`; no class, async, await, yield, global or nonlocal statements, no star or relative imports; no `http(s)://`, `ftp://`, `file:` or `data:` literals; string literals at most 200 characters and at most 2,000 characters of new literal text per plan; every call of a save name (`regions.SAVE_NAMES`: `savefig`, `save`, `ggsave`, and since 2026-10-26 `render_to_png`, `render_to_file` and `svg2png`) is checked against the library's save rule (`regions.SAVE_RULES`, which replaced the savefig rule on 2026-10-25): a name the rule does not list is a `banned-call` (`np.save` with matplotlib; `save` itself left the banned I/O names for this rule), the file argument (the rule's positional, `fname=`, `filename=` or `fp=`, or `write_to=` for `svg2png`, which takes every argument by keyword; `ggsave` takes the plot first and the file second) is exactly `f"plot-{THEME}.png"`, for altair, pygal and lets-plot also `f"plot-{THEME}.html"`, lets-plot's `ggsave` passes exactly `path="."` (`SaveRule.directory`), a save takes no `**` arguments, plotnine's `save` and `ggsave` and altair's `save` take only the keywords the rule lists (the size, the dpi, altair's `scale_factor` and embedding options; `path=`, `webdriver=` and `engine=` are findings), pygal's `render_to_png` takes only `filename` and `dpi` and `render_to_file` only `filename` (their `**kwargs` reach the chart's config), each pygal save name writes only its own file type (`render_to_png` and `svg2png` the PNG, `render_to_file` the HTML; `SaveRule.writes`), `svg2png` never takes `url=`, `file_obj=` or `unsafe=` (they read through cairosvg's unrestricted fetcher), a file without a save of the PNG is a finding, and a save name or `.open` is only ever called where it is named (`f = plot.save; f("x")` would hide the target); since 2026-10-26 a save name imported under another name (`from plotnine import ggsave as keep`), anything imported under a save name, and a bare save name that is not a call's callee (`g = ggsave`, `[ggsave][0](...)`, `functools.partial(ggsave, plot)`) are a `banned-call` in every profile, which closes a route the plotnine profile of 2026-10-25 left open in the exported code (the plot guard refused the write in the sandbox); since the same day `THEME`, part of every save target, keeps its theme block's value in every profile: any binding of it but the first module-level theme block after `import os` is a `savefig-target` finding, and any binding of `os` but that import an `os-use` finding; `.open` is admitted only in altair's profile and only on the PNG, for the catalogue's PIL padding block (`Image.open(f"plot-{THEME}.png")`, then `_canvas.save(f"plot-{THEME}.png")`, which the save rule checks); `def` and `lambda` stay allowed for formatters. The ADAPTATION rules run on adapted code only: exactly one placeholder; no RNG used to create data (a seeded `np.random.default_rng(<int literal>)` is allowed, which the prompt and the repair line keep to jitter since 2026-11-09, and the row-fidelity gate catches fabricated data); numeric literal lists of at most 20 items; the palette prefix preserved. SECURITY failures reject the code and get a repair attempt while one is allowed; ADAPTATION failures become defect lines. Every bypass named here is a validator-corpus test. The review of 2026-10-14 found that the validator, a list of banned names, accepted `np.testing.measure("...")`, which compiles and runs any string, so a survey walked every public function of the allowed packages for `exec`, `eval`, `compile`, processes, sockets, fetches, import by name and pickle, and every module they hand out as an attribute; its routes are closed by kind, not one by one: submodules that test, build, start programs or import by name (`testing`, `tests`, `distutils`, `backends`, `animation`, `texmanager`, `dviread`, `sphinxext`, `pylab`, pandas' `computation` and `compat`, seaborn's `external`, sklearn's `externals`, `discovery` and `estimator_checks`, statsmodels' `x13` and `iolib`) are banned like the re-exports, as attributes, `from` imports and import path components, and a test pins that every public first-level submodule of numpy, pandas, matplotlib, seaborn, scikit-learn, the toolkits and the admitted statsmodels packages is either reviewed as safe or banned, so an upgrade that adds one fails until someone reviews it; single helpers (`test()`, which runs pytest, `add_newdoc` and `import_module`, `switch_backend`, `register_backend`, `TexManager`, `math_to_image`, `thumbnail`, `json_dump`, the seaborn and statsmodels fetchers, `datasets`, the `print_*` canvas writers and the `x13_*` runners) are banned like `.show`; modules re-exported as attributes (`mpl.tempfile`, `mpl.logging`, pickle, pkgutil, pydoc, the file, archive and network modules, `sklearn.<module>.scipy`) joined the re-export list; and an attribute path resolved through an import must stay inside the import surface, so `scipy.datasets.face()` after `import scipy.stats` is refused. Settings that start programs or import by name are their own rule (`rc-key`): a dotted string literal naming `text.usetex`, `text.latex.*`, `pgf.*`, `animation.*` (the ffmpeg and ImageMagick paths), `ps.usedistiller`, `webagg.*`, a key ending `_path`, or `backend` (pandas' `plotting.backend` too) is a finding anywhere, and in a settings position (an `rcParams` subscript, a key of a settings dict, a `**{...}` keyword) the bare `backend` and `usetex` are too; `rcParams` is written only with a literal key or `.update` with a dict literal, `rc_context` and `rc=` take literal dicts and no settings file (`fname`), `style.context` takes a literal dict or a style name, never a path to a style sheet, `rc(group, ...)` takes a literal group; the keywords `usetex=` and `backend=` are banned on every call, the value `"TeX"` (`ismath="TeX"`) is a finding, and a save takes no `format=` but its file's own type (`"png"`, altair's `"html"`); plotnine's `theme_matplotlib(rc=...)` follows the `rc_context` rules, literal keys and no settings file. The cache and spill directories of scikit-learn and joblib are banned too (Fable review of #12177, after a survey of the admitted libraries' public signatures): the keywords `memory=` (`Pipeline`, `make_pipeline`, `AgglomerativeClustering`, `FeatureAgglomeration`, `OPTICS`), `temp_folder=` and `cachedir=` on every call, also as string keys of a dict literal unpacked into a call, and the helpers `Memory`, `check_memory` and `Parallel`, whose cache or spill directory is a positional parameter; `location=` stays allowed, because matplotlib's colorbar and secondary axes take it as a side. The render guard refuses those writes in the sandbox; the rule keeps them out of the exported code, which runs unguarded on the user's machine. A key built at run time still passes these rules, which is why the harness arms its plot guard (see [Render](#render)). plotnine and altair (step 1b of the phase-2 plan, 2026-10-25) brought routes of their own; their public submodules were walked on 2026-10-24 and the walk was checked adversarially on 2026-10-25, and `validate.py`'s module docstring and sets list every name. Three of them go beyond a banned name. plotnine compiles and evaluates a string that is not a column name wherever it takes an aesthetic or a facet, with real builtins and the plot's namespace, so `aes(x='open("/etc/passwd").read()')` is code: every string literal passed to `aes`, `after_stat`, `after_scale`, `stage`, `facet_wrap`, `facet_grid` or `qplot` (`EXPRESSION_CALLS`) is parsed as a Python expression, each side and term of a facet formula on its own, and walked with this profile's name, attribute, call and dunder rules; a bare column name and a text that does not parse (a column such as "True Label") pass, and those calls take `**` only from a dict literal and `*` only from a list or tuple literal, so every string they evaluate is in the walk. `formula=` is a banned keyword on every call, because statsmodels hands it to patsy, which `exec`s Python while it parses (`stat_smooth`, `stat_quantile`; the render guard refuses that `exec`). And altair's image mark is banned (`mark_image`, `Url*` and the keyword `url=`), because vl-convert's SVG renderer reads a `file://` href from disk in native code the audit hook never sees, together with `Chart.from_dict` and `from_json` in altair's profile only (`LIBRARY_BANS`), whose spec the string rules cannot read. Among the names: every plotnine plot's `.environment`, whose namespace holds the `os` module as a value; altair's `limit_rows`, whose narwhals frame hands out the pyarrow module, `transformed_data`, which loads `url` data in VegaFusion, and its plugin and renderer registries; and the `pathlib.Path` and polars writers among the banned I/O names. pygal and lets-plot (step 1c, 2026-10-26) got the same walk of their public submodules, pinned by the same test (pygal 3.1.3, cairosvg 2.9.1, lets-plot 4.11.0), and rules of their own. libcairo writes pygal's PNG with its own `fopen`, which the audit hook never sees, so the static pin of the save target, not the plot guard, holds `render_to_png` and `svg2png` to the plot file. cairosvg's CSS stack builds code from the chart's stylesheet (`CODE_BUILDERS`, see [Render](#render)), so `cssselect2`, `tinycss2`, `cairocffi` and cairosvg's `css`, `parser`, `surface`, `url` and `image` modules are unreachable, and pygal's config keywords have a rule: `defs=` is refused, `js=` takes only an empty list, `css=` takes only inline rules and pygal's three package stylesheets (`file://style.css` and its siblings, exempt from the URL rule in that position alone), and `.css` and `.js` are only ever the single target of a plain assignment. Every `**` in pygal's profile is a dict literal with string keys or a name bound once and used only that way, a `googlefont:` value counts as a URL literal, and the SVG namespace names (`http://www.w3.org/2000/svg` and `http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink`) are exempt from the URL rule in pygal's profile only. lets-plot's geocoding, map tiles, livemap and browser contexts are denied (the modules above and 23 helpers such as `geocode` and `to_png`), `LetsPlot` is admitted only as `LetsPlot.setup_html()` with no arguments (`LetsPlot.set`, `setup_show_ext` and `cfg` are refused), and `.props` is banned. The `.format` ban has one exemption, in lets-plot's profile only: a `.format` with string-literal arguments at the end of a chain of `layer_tooltips()`, `layer_labels()` or `smooth_labels()` builder methods, each of which returns the builder, imported from `lets_plot` under a name bound once (59 catalogue files use it, and 53 of them are eligible only through it). The plotnine expression rule changed too: a name it reads as a module-level string must be bound exactly once, match captures included, so `case COLUMN:` can no longer rebind it to code.

**The R (ggplot2) profile.** Since 2026-10-29, `agents/anyplot/code/profiles/r/` holds the same steps for R, read with tree-sitter (`code/treesitter.py`; `tree-sitter` 0.26.0 and `tree-sitter-language-pack` 0.13.0, which bundles its grammars, and the agents image keeps only the grammars of its profiles, R, since J2a Julia, and since JS-3 JavaScript and TSX). `RRuntime` (`render/runtimes/r.py`) binds it for the pipeline, so the same steps above run for ggplot2 in R. The profile works as follows:

- The placeholder is `df <- load_user_data()`, and the loader replaces it with `readr::read_csv("data.csv", col_types = readr::cols(...), na = "", ...)`, each column typed from its kind in `data.csv` (`data/kinds.py`, which the pipeline reads once per run and hands every runtime's `loader_block`). An integer column past 32 bits is read as a double, which is exact only up to 2^53. The runtime's `loader_notes` names the columns with integer cells beyond that and how many, and the pipeline puts that line first in the shipped version's "what changed" list, which the page shows and the root names in its reply (owner rule of 2026-10-15: no silent loss). Owner decision of 2026-10-29: "what changed", not a residual note, so the status stays the render's. A timestamp with a UTC offset is read in UTC, so the plot shows another clock time (`08:00+01:00` as `07:00`): since 2026-10-30 a second line names the columns with offsets other than zero and how many (`ColumnKinds.offset`, worded once in `runtimes/notes.offset_note` for R, Julia and the JavaScript runtime to come).
- ADAPTATION rejects a working form that keeps a dataset package (`dataset-package`, gapminder or palmerpenguins): the R runtime's blocking rules add it to the placeholder rules, while the readiness scan still calls a catalogue file with one `coupled`. The targets of the other ADAPTATION findings are written in R (`RuntimeAdapter.adaptation_targets`).
- The adapter reads `adapter.md`, whose rules every language shares, with the R passages of `adapter_r.md` in its slots: the placeholder and how the loader types the columns, column constants read through `.data[[...]]`, the protected statements, the packages it may attach (never a dataset package or `readr`) and what the validator refuses. The Python libraries read the Python passages (`adapter_python.md`), and their prompt is byte for byte what it was before the split.
- The protected regions are `THEME <- Sys.getenv("ANYPLOT_THEME", "light")`, the theme tokens, and the final `ggsave(filename = sprintf("plot-%s.png", THEME), device = ragg::agg_png, ...)`.
- The normaliser strips the catalogue header, empties the catalogue title, and changes only the save's `dpi`, so every catalogue file renders 3200x1800 or 2400x2400. ragg truncates `inches * dpi`, and the normaliser plans for that.
- The validator refuses a denied name (process, file, network, evaluation and string-to-function routes) as a callee or a function-valued argument everywhere, and as any other value outside a data mask. Inside `aes()`, `vars()`, the facets, the dplyr and tidyr verbs, and the operands of `~`, a bare name is a column, so a user's column called `source` or `file` passes. A string equal to a denied name is refused only where R turns it into a function. Strings that ggplot2 or R parse as code (facets, formulas) are walked as code, and a formula built at run time is refused. The rules are hygiene for the exported `plot.R`; the sandbox and the exec filter are the boundary.
- The readiness scan blocks only for real reasons: a map spec, a security finding, no theme statement or no protected save. A file without the `# Data` marker is `coupled`, never `blocked`. Of the 325 catalogue files, 4 are clean, 306 coupled and 15 blocked (the 13 map specs, qrcode-basic and maze-circular).
- The error summary reads R's stderr for the erroring function, its cause and the last calls, and never quotes the message.

**The Julia (Makie) profile.** Since 2026-10-29, `agents/anyplot/code/profiles/julia/` holds the same steps for Julia, read with the same pinned tree-sitter pack. Since 2026-10-30, `JuliaRuntime` (`render/runtimes/julia.py`) binds it for the pipeline (J2b of [the phase-2 plan](../../agentic/specs/261024-agent-all-libraries.md), step 5). The profile works as follows:

- The placeholder is `df = load_user_data()`, at the top level only, and the loader replaces it with `import CSV, DataFrames, ...` and `CSV.read("data.csv", DataFrames.DataFrame; types = Dict(...), missingstring = "", strict = true, ...)`, each column typed from its kind. Integer cells past 32 bits are read as text and parsed with Julia's own `parse(Int64, ...)`, which is exact over the whole range, because CSV.jl 0.10.17 reads -9223372036854775807 to -9223372036854775800 without their sign. Timestamps go through TimeZones.jl: a cell with an offset or `Z` becomes UTC, as readr does, and a fraction is truncated to milliseconds, which is all Julia's `DateTime` holds. `data/kinds.py` counts the truncated cells per column for the runtime to announce, and the cells with a UTC offset other than zero, which the loader reads in UTC, for a second line worded as R's.
- The protected regions are `THEME = get(ENV, "ANYPLOT_THEME", "light")` (with or without `const`), the theme tokens, and the final `save("plot-$(THEME).png", fig; px_per_unit = 2)`.
- The normaliser strips the catalogue header, empties the catalogue title, rewrites the four saves next to the script (`joinpath(@__DIR__, ...)`) to the plain file name, renames the deprecated `resolution` keyword to `size`, and changes only the save's `px_per_unit` when a figure is off target. CairoMakie rounds the figure size to whole units and then the pixels, and the normaliser plans for that.
- The validator refuses by shape: a denied name (process starts, file I/O, `ENV` outside the theme statement, evaluation, reflection such as `getfield`, native code) anywhere it is read, also as a member after a `.`; a package used as a value; a call through a computed value; macros and string macros outside short lists; command literals; `using` outside the project's plotting packages and the standard libraries the catalogue uses. String interpolation is walked as code. The rules are hygiene for the exported `plot.jl`; the sandbox and the exec filter are the boundary.
- The readiness scan blocks only for real reasons. Of the 325 catalogue files, none is clean (no file has column constants yet), 308 are coupled and 17 blocked: the 13 map specs, qrcode-basic, which needs a package outside the project, and three files that save inside a `with_theme` block.
- The error summary reads Julia's stderr for the exception type, the plot's line and the function it called there, and never quotes the message. Makie's stacks run to 7,500 characters and more, so the Julia row keeps Julia's first `ERROR:` line in front of the 2,000-character stderr tail (`RuntimeCommand.stderr_head`, `executor.stderr_tail`; every other row's tail is the plain last characters). The loader replaces the one placeholder line with 15 lines and more, so the summary takes the loader's span off the line numbers and names the line of the working form, which the adapter edits; a line inside the loader is "the data loader".
- When the loader cuts a timestamp's digits below the millisecond, the first entry of the version's "what changed" list names the columns with their counts (`JuliaRuntime.loader_notes`), and the plot's status stays the render's, as for R's integers past 2^53.
- The adapter's Julia passages (`prompts/adapter_julia.md`) teach the placeholder and the loader's types, column constants read as `df[!, COL]` (no catalogue Makie file builds a DataFrame), the protected statements, the packages the adapter may load (never `CSV` or `TimeZones`, the loader's alone), a number formatter with the plot language's marks, and what Makie 0.24 refuses that the catalogue never meets: a `Date` column with a `missing` cell, spans and bands on a time axis, and text as `barplot!`'s x.
- The export's run line is the catalogue's own, `julia --project=. plot.jl`.

**plotly and bokeh.** Since 2026-10-31 (PB-2a of [the phase-2 plan](../../agentic/specs/261024-agent-all-libraries.md), step 4) the Python runtime serves both through the renderer's figure mode, and the admin deploy names them. Their code is Python like the other six, with three differences:

- The run form ends on one pinned statement that writes the figure as JSON (`agents/anyplot/code/figure.py`): `fig.update_layout(width=800, height=450).write_json(f"plot-{THEME}.json")` for plotly, whose size the normaliser moves there from the catalogue's `write_image`, and `json.dump(json_item(p), figure_file)` in a `with open(f"plot-{THEME}.json", "w", encoding="utf-8") as figure_file:` block for bokeh. The normaliser writes it in place of the catalogue's save block (bokeh's Selenium screenshot block included), the regions protect it, and the validator takes exactly that shape out of its scan, so `open`, `json` and `write_json` stay refused everywhere else. The figure mode draws it at the canvas by the device scale; the export swaps it for the library's normal save (see [Fencing and exported code](#fencing-and-exported-code)).
- The validator admits plotly's `graph_objects`, `express`, `subplots`, `colors` and `figure_factory` (never `plotly.io`), and bokeh's plotting, models, layouts, palettes, transforms and colours; it refuses bokeh's JavaScript models and callbacks (`CustomJS...`, `js_on_event`), its markup models (`Div`, the HTML labels), its import by name and every model that loads from a URL or a file, plotly's geo and map traces, which load their maps from the network, and in both a private name (`_validate=False`) and a string where a URL is expected, whatever its scheme (owner decisions of 2026-10-31). The exported HTML runs what the code builds in the user's browser without the render page's policy.
- bokeh's tooltips are HTML: BokehJS parses a template string and each pair's value with the DOM parser and escapes only the `@field` values it inserts. The validator reads every text a `tooltips` value can take and admits templates as the catalogue writes them, text tags with quoted `style` or `class` attributes that load nothing, never `{safe}`; a value it cannot read, such as a Python variable's text, is refused, and so is a `**` argument other than literal options, which could carry a keyword built at run time.
- The validator reads code, and code can build a `<` at run time (`chr(60)`). So before a figure page opens, the renderer's harness reads the figure the plot wrote and refuses what a browser would run, load or link, whatever built it (`figure UnsafeMarkup`): bokeh's JavaScript, HTML, CSS and URL models and any model no catalogue plot uses, JavaScript callbacks given as properties, MathJax's HTML commands, a tooltip that breaks the template rule, and in plotly any link, styles that load, and image or `geojson` URLs. The export is the same code on the same data, so what the render refused never reaches the downloaded `plot.py`'s HTML.
- A plotly WebGL trace (3D, `Scattergl`, `Parcoords`, the map traces) is blocked by the readiness scan and fails the render as `draw NoWebGL`, since the renderer's headless browser draws no WebGL yet; plotly express gets `render_mode="svg"`. Of the catalogue's 325 files each, 273 plotly and 252 bokeh files are eligible.

The adapter reads a section for each library after the Python prompt (`prompts/adapter_plotly.md`, `adapter_bokeh.md`): the protected save, the theme tokens on the figure, timestamps in UTC, plotly's `separators` for the plot language's marks, and bokeh's ticks on fixed values with labels written in Python, because BokehJS's number formatters write English marks.

**The JavaScript profile (Chart.js, D3, ECharts, Highcharts, MUI X).** Since 2026-10-30, `agents/anyplot/code/profiles/javascript/` holds the same steps for the five JavaScript libraries, read with the pack's `javascript` grammar and, for MUI X's `.tsx` modules, its `tsx` grammar. Since 2026-10-30 `JavaScriptRuntime` (`render/runtimes/javascript.py`, JS-4a of [the phase-2 plan](../../agentic/specs/261024-agent-all-libraries.md), step 3) binds it for the pipeline, and the admin deploy serves Chart.js, D3, ECharts and Highcharts, and MUI X since 2026-10-31 (JS-4c), whose code is a TSX module that the harness bundles with React through its closed import plugin and mounts as a component. The code runs in the JavaScript harness's page, which sets the theme, the tokens and the canvas and saves the screenshot. The profile works as follows:

- The placeholder is `const data = loadUserData();`, at the top level only, and the loader replaces it with `const data = window.ANYPLOT_DATA;`: the table the harness injects, one object per row with `data.columns`, which Python types from the column kinds before the page (`data/typed_json.py`). One line replaces one line, so error lines need no shift.
- The protected regions are the token lines (top-level declarations that read `window.ANYPLOT_TOKENS`, `window.ANYPLOT_THEME` or `window.ANYPLOT_SIZE`, and those that pick a value per theme through an earlier token) and the orientation directive `//# anyplot-orientation: square` or `landscape`, which picks the canvas. The edits' drift check keeps the directive although it is a comment.
- The normaliser strips the catalogue header, writes exactly one directive on the first line (the orientation the harness reads now, landscape when the file has none), and empties the catalogue title, in JSX text too. It leaves MUI X files written for MUI X 7.29 as they are.
- The validator resolves JavaScript's scopes and admits a closed world of global names: the built-ins that compute, `window` and `document` only as the object of a named member from a short list, and the file's own library global. The network, storage, frames, workers, `eval`, `Function`, `import()`, `require`, `.constructor`, timers with a string, elements that run or load (`script`, `iframe`, ...) and writes to the tokens are refused, also through an alias, a shorthand property, optional chaining or a literal index. Since the review of JS-3, so are `this` used as a value where it may be the window (a function called without a receiver), a key built from strings that could spell `constructor` and its kind, protocol-relative URLs, writes of `src` and `href` with anything but a data URL the code drew (`canvas.toDataURL()`), any write of `location`, `action` or `cookie`, and `.write()`: the exported page runs in the user's browser, where a content security policy stops a request but not a navigation. MUI X imports only the modules of the renderer image's import allowlist, of which the profile carries a copy that a test and CI compare with the image's. The rules are hygiene for the exported file; the harness's page rules and the sandbox are the boundary, and the shapes that still pass are pinned as tests.
- The readiness scan blocks only for real reasons. Of the 1,617 catalogue files, 93 MUI X files regenerated for MUI X 9 are clean, 1,456 coupled and 68 blocked: the map specs, qrcode-basic, which encodes a URL, and one Chart.js file with a 683-character HTML string. A MUI X file that imports what MUI X 9 no longer exports (`@mui/x-charts/ChartContainer`, 51 files) is coupled with a note that names the import (owner decision of 2026-10-30), and the adapter rewrites it (`ChartsContainer`, overlays through the chart's scales); of the 323 MUI X files 311 are eligible.
- The error summary reads the harness's final error line (`render: error: <kind> <Class> <file>:<line>: ...`) for the kind, the class and the line, and never quotes the message. With the run form it names a line on the loader as "the data loader" and leaves out a line past the code's end, which is the harness's wrapper (a thrown value that is no `Error`).
- The table reaches the page as typed JSON: the pipeline builds it once per run from the canonical CSV and its column kinds (`RuntimeAdapter.data_json`, `data/typed_json.py`), every render job of the run and the shipped version carry it (`RenderJob.data_json`, `CodeVersion.data_json`), so the theme toggle renders the other theme from the same table, and the backend writes it as `data.json` next to the code file and names it in `ANYPLOT_DATA_JSON`. A JavaScript number is a double and a `Date` holds milliseconds, so the first entries of the version's "what changed" list count the integers past 2^53, the timestamps cut to the millisecond and the timestamps converted to UTC, per column (`JavaScriptRuntime.loader_notes`); the status stays the render's.
- The adapter's JavaScript passages (`prompts/adapter_javascript.md`) teach the placeholder and how the table is typed, column constants read as `d[COL]`, the protected token lines and directive, `null` dropped before any conversion or statistic (`+null` is `0`), a number formatter with the plot language's marks for every library's value axis (Highcharts writes `8k` otherwise), time axes kept in UTC so the exported page shows the same dates in any time zone, and Chart.js without a time axis (the page has no date adapter). For MUI X (since 2026-10-31) they also teach the module's imports and default export, MUI X 9.16's names for MUI X 7's (`ChartContainer` is `ChartsContainer`), axis bands sized for the labels, overlays drawn through the chart's scales (`@mui/x-charts/hooks`) instead of pixel positions from `margin`, a `"utc"` time axis with a formatter (a `"time"` axis follows the browser's zone), and a series formatter that guards the `null` of a gap, which MUI X hands it on hover. Each idiom passes both validator profiles and renders in the renderer image (`tests/unit/agents/code/test_profile_javascript_image.py`).
- The renderer's deploy smoke renders a Chart.js chart from a typed table and refuses the promotion when it fails, as the Makie render does.
- The export's header uses `//` comments, and its run line names the self-contained `plot.html` that the export carries next to the code file (owner decision of 2026-10-30), because the code runs only in that page. Since 2026-10-31 (JS-4b-1) a JavaScript version offers that page as the artifact `plot.html` and the result card downloads it with `.download('plot.html')` in the theme on screen. The agents service builds it on demand from the version's run form and typed table through the renderer's `POST /page`, where the harness writes the page a render would screenshot and starts no browser (`ANYPLOT_PAGE_ONLY=1`), and wraps it: a document with no script of its own and the harness's content security policy plus `frame-src 'none'`, the page in `<iframe sandbox="allow-scripts" srcdoc=...>`, scaled by CSS to fit the window. The plot code then runs in an opaque origin that can neither navigate the window nor open popups, fetch, or read cookies and storage, which a probe checks in the renderer image's CI (`agents/renderer/page_probe.mjs`) for a Chart.js page and, since JS-4c, a MUI X page. Since JS-4b-2 (2026-10-31, route A of `agentic/specs/261024-agent-all-libraries.md`, the owner's decision) `.interactive()` shows the same page in the card: the chat fetches it through the authenticated artifact route and posts it into a relay page on the API, `GET /preview/relay` (`api/preview_relay.py`), framed with `sandbox="allow-scripts"`. The relay is static and unauthenticated, takes no token and reads no cookie; its own policy (`sandbox allow-scripts`, the harness's policy, `frame-ancestors` the site's origins and local dev servers) gives it an opaque origin without network. It posts one constant ready signal without content to its parent, on which alone the card posts the page, so a stand-in at its URL (an error page of the edge) never receives it, and it writes the first page its parent posts from an allowed origin into a sandboxed `srcdoc` frame. The same probe checks the relay in the image's Chromium.

### Render

`agents/anyplot/render/` defines `RenderJob{job_id, language, library, source, data_csv, themes, timeout_s, data_json}` (`data_json` for a JavaScript job only, since 2026-10-30) and two protocols: `RuntimeAdapter` (one per library, from the registry `runtimes.runtime_for`: `file_name`, `command` as the CI command verbatim, `env`, `loader_block`, `normalise`, `validate`, `collect`, and since 2026-10-24 the language's code conventions the pipeline checks against (`regions`, `readiness`, `security`, `adaptation`, `apply_plan`, `restore_protected`, `new_literals`), its error grammar (`error_summary`), its export with the code artifact's name (`export`, `code_artifact`: `plot.py`, `plot.R`, `plot.jl`, `plot.js`, `plot.tsx`, which `schemas.ArtifactName`, the artifact route and the feedback case follow) and the render time a repair reserves (`render_p95_s`), and since 2026-10-29 the ADAPTATION rules that reject the code (`blocking_rules`), the targets of the others (`adaptation_targets`), the column kinds `loader_block` takes and what its loader changes in the data (`loader_notes`), and whether the reviewer checks the requested texts of a render without drawn texts (`review_texts`)) and `RenderBackend` with the backends `remote` (phase 1: the `anyplot-renderer` service, `AGENT_RENDERER=remote` with `AGENT_RENDER_URL`), `sandbox` (sandboxes launched inside the agents service; a stub that phase 1 does not use), `local` (development only; refuses `ENVIRONMENT=production`; always `docker run --rm --network none --read-only --tmpfs /tmp -v <rundir>:/work <renderer image> /app/.venv/bin/python -I /opt/anyplot/harness.py plot.py` with the production renderer image, fails if Docker is missing, no bare-subprocess fallback) and `fake` (unit tests, fixture PNGs). The registry serves twelve libraries and picks the runtime class by the library's language (`RUNTIME_CLASSES`). matplotlib, seaborn, plotnine, altair, pygal and lets-plot (`letsplot`) go through `PythonRuntime` (plotnine and altair since 2026-10-25, pygal and lets-plot since 2026-10-26), which hands its library to the regions, the readiness scan, the edits, the normaliser and the validator, so each library gets its own save rule and import surface. plotnine draws through matplotlib, so its renders carry the probe and its advisory gates. altair renders in vl-convert's embedded V8, pygal through cairosvg and libcairo, and lets-plot in its Kotlin/Native bridge (ImageMagick linked in): none of them draws a matplotlib figure, so the host gates alone judge them, and since 2026-11-01 their probe reports the drawn texts alone, for the reply. ggplot2 goes through `RRuntime` since 2026-10-29: the R code profile, `plot.R` run by the renderer's R row in exec mode under the preloaded exec filter, the PNG and (since 2026-11-01) a probe of the drawn texts alone, R's error grammar, the export with `Rscript plot.R` in its run line, a render reserve of 15 s like Python's (the first deployed R render took 5.7 s of wall time on Cloud Run), and Python's data runs and conversions, because `run_data_code` runs Python whatever the plot's language. The local Docker backend renders it with the row's environment, key for key the renderer's. Makie goes through `JuliaRuntime` since 2026-10-30 in the same way: the Julia code profile, `plot.jl` run by the renderer's Julia row in exec mode under the preloaded exec filter, the PNG and (since 2026-11-01) a probe of the drawn texts alone, Julia's error grammar over a stderr tail that keeps the first `ERROR:` line, the export with `julia --project=. plot.jl`, a render reserve of 25 s from the Julia row, and Python's data runs and conversions. Chart.js, D3, ECharts and Highcharts go through `JavaScriptRuntime` since 2026-10-30: the JavaScript code profile, `plot.js` run by the renderer's JavaScript row (Node and the catalogue's harness in exec mode, no exec filter, the harness's sandbox mode), the job's typed table as `data.json`, the PNG and (since 2026-11-01) a probe of the drawn texts alone, the harness's final error line, the export whose run line names `plot.html`, a render reserve of 15 s, and Python's data runs and conversions. Since 2026-10-31 `RenderBackend.page(PageJob)` builds a JavaScript version's page for the `plot.html` download: the `remote` backend posts it to the renderer's `POST /page` and reads a `404` as `PageUnsupported`, because the agents service deploys before the renderer; the `local` backend runs a render's container with `ANYPLOT_PAGE_ONLY=1` and mounts the checkout's `automation/js-render/render.mjs` over the image's for every JavaScript container, renders included, as it mounts the checkout's `harness.py`; `fake` returns a stub page.

The renderer is a service of its own (owner decision of 2026-10-10), so the deployed agents service and a developer's `adk web` render in the same sandboxes. `agents/renderer/` has no ADK import and no `__init__.py` (`adk web agents` would list it as an agent). `POST /render` takes the `RenderJob` as JSON and answers per theme the exit code, a timeout flag, a stderr tail of at most 2,000 characters, the probe, the PNG as base64 (at most 10 MB), the attempts and a reason (`timeout`, `disk_budget`, `file_budget`, `memory`, `launcher` or `output_rejected`) with the measured value and the limit, plus the slot wait, the total time and `MemAvailable`; `POST /run-data` (since 2026-10-14) takes `{job_id, source, timeout_s}` (at most 16 KB of source and 30 s) and runs one data snippet as `snippet.py` with the harness in data mode (`harness.py --data`), in the same render slot, under the same memory floor, watchdog, kill path and launcher retry, with no theme and no matplotlib environment; it answers `{exit_code, timed_out, reason, measured, limit, csv_text, stderr_tail, wall_s, attempts}` plus the timings, the version and `MemAvailable`, where `csv_text` is the `out.csv` the harness wrote, read only after exit code 0 with `read_regular` under the 2 MB cap of computed data as strict UTF-8 (anything else is `output_rejected`). `POST /convert` (since 2026-10-15) takes `{job_id, data_base64, sheet?, timeout_s}` (at most 5 MiB of file and 15 s) and reads one binary data file with the harness in convert mode (`harness.py --convert input.bin`, the converter `convert.py` beside the harness, Arrow's system allocator), in the same slot and under the same limits; it answers `{exit_code, timed_out, reason, refusal, measured, limit, text, meta, wall_s, attempts}`, where `text` (at most 1 MiB) and `meta` (`wire.ConvertMeta`: kind, format, rows, columns, sheet, at most ten sheet names, warning counts) are read only after exit code 0 (see the conversion under [Request flow](#request-flow-for-use-with-my-data)). `POST /page` (since 2026-10-31, JS-4b) takes `{job_id, language, library, source, data_json, theme, timeout_s}` for a JavaScript job and runs the JavaScript row with `ANYPLOT_PAGE_ONLY=1` in the same slot and under the same limits: the harness builds the page a render would screenshot, writes `plot-<theme>.html` and starts no browser, so the plot code does not run in the sandbox; the answer is that page as `text/html` (at most 24 MiB, `wire.MAX_PAGE_BYTES`), or `422 page_failed` with the exit code, the reason and the stderr tail, and a finished page is not kept for replays. In data mode the harness imports everything the DATA profile allows, every module of `pandas.core`, `pandas.tseries` and `pandas.api` (pandas loads some lazily) and every time zone, reads and compiles the snippet, opens `out.csv`, and then arms an audit hook (`sys.addaudithook`) that refuses, for the rest of the process, every `open` (reading too), every `import` of a module not loaded yet, `ctypes.*`, `os.system`, `os.exec*`, `os.posix_spawn`, `os.spawn*`, `os.fork*`, `subprocess.*`, `socket.*`, every filesystem change and listing (`os.listdir`, `os.scandir`), every route to a frame (`gi_frame`, `cr_frame`, `ag_frame`, `tb_frame`, `sys._getframe`), because a frame's `f_builtins` hands out `open` and `__import__` past the validator (the Fable security review of 2026-10-14 ran `(i for i in range(1)).gi_frame.f_builtins['open']` end to end), and every `exec` and `compile` but the snippet's own run and three code builders that fill a fixed template with names they checked as identifiers (`namedtuple`, which `DataFrame.itertuples()` uses, dataclasses, scipy's distributions). CPython raises no audit event for `os.stat`, reading `os.environ` or `function.__globals__`, nor for importing a module that is loaded already; those stay the validator's; it takes `df` from the run's globals (a DataFrame, no MultiIndex columns, a non-default index reset into columns, names as text, at most 20,000 rows, 50 columns and 2 MB of CSV, 200 KB until 2026-10-14) and writes it through the handle opened before the hook. The snippet never chooses the exit code (the harness catches everything it raises, `SystemExit` included), each refusal has its own code, and a refusal the snippet caught still refuses the run. The plot mode arms an audit hook of its own (`PlotGuard`, since 2026-10-14), after the limits, the font cache and the probe are set up and the plot file is compiled: it refuses every `exec` but the plot's own run, the module bodies of an import (an import frame met before any plot frame, walking up from the caller) and the code builders (`CODE_BUILDERS`: the three above, plotnine's `Environment.eval` since 2026-10-25, and since 2026-10-26 cssselect2's selector compiler, which pygal's PNG export runs once per CSS selector of the chart and which quotes every string the stylesheet controls with `repr`); a code object or function built by hand and a `__code__` assignment; every `open` that writes anything but `plot-<theme>.png`, `probe-<theme>.json` and, since 2026-10-25, `plot-<theme>.html` (the second file of altair, and since 2026-10-26 of pygal and lets-plot, which stays in the sandbox: no backend collects it; libcairo writes pygal's PNG natively, past this hook, so the validator's pin holds that path); `subprocess.*`, `os.system`, `os.exec*`, `os.posix_spawn`, `os.spawn*`, `os.fork*`, `socket.*`, `ctypes.*` (except threadpoolctl's, which scikit-learn uses to limit BLAS threads), filesystem changes and `os.chdir`; and, since the Fable review of #12177, every route to a frame (`gi_frame`, `cr_frame`, `ag_frame`, `tb_frame`, `sys._getframe`, `sys._current_frames`) while plot code is on the stack, so neither the plot nor library code it called (`pd.Series([g]).apply(getattr, args=("gi_frame",))`) reaches a frame's `f_builtins`. Two kinds of frame reader stay allowed: an import body (an import frame met before any plot frame, as for `exec`), and a short list of library functions that read a name or a value and return no frame (`FRAME_READERS`: pandas' chained-assignment check, the caller lookup of statsmodels' graphics, and `traceback.extract_stack` and `extract_tb`, which pyparsing calls while matplotlib builds and runs its mathtext parser, and through which the interpreter prints a failed plot's traceback, so the error line still reaches the repair; and since 2026-10-25 plotnine's `Environment.capture`, which walks from `inspect.currentframe()` to the plot's own frame and keeps its `f_locals` and `f_globals`, the namespace the plot has anyway, to look column names up in `aes("x")`; every plotnine file failed without it with `call-stack is not that deep!`). A stack with no plot frame gets no frame either: a worker thread runs a callable the plot submitted (`ThreadPoolExecutor.submit(sys._current_frames)`) with no plot frame above it, and the frames it returned reached `run_plot`'s locals and let the plot disarm the guard (#12181 review). For the same reason both guards refuse `gc.get_objects`, `gc.get_referrers` and `gc.get_referents`, which hand out the guard object itself, and every callback the interpreter hands a real frame (Fable review of #12181, reproduced in both modes): `sys.settrace` and `sys.setprofile` (also raised by `threading`'s `settrace`, `setprofile` and their `_all_threads` forms) and `sys.monitoring.register_callback`, the one PEP 669 call with an audit event. A trace function walks `f_back`, which raises no event, to the locals of `run_plot` or `run_data_snippet`, where the guard sits. `signal.signal`, whose handler gets the interrupted frame and which raises no event, is replaced by a refusal before arming, like `os.mkfifo`. The guard's own state stays a plain attribute: CPython gives in-process code no state that a reference cannot reach, so the defence is that no route hands the code under test a reference. The validator bans `settrace`, `setprofile`, `settrace_all_threads`, `setprofile_all_threads` and `monitoring` as attributes on any object and as `from` imports as a second layer. `inspect.currentframe` and `logging.currentframe`, which return their caller's frame, are replaced before the guard is armed: `logging.currentframe` answers None, as in data mode, and since 2026-10-25 `inspect.currentframe` answers its caller's frame to a `FRAME_READERS` entry and None to every other caller (`frame_for_readers`, a `sys._getframe(1)` the guard judges like any other frame route, with a refusal turned into None), while data mode keeps None for every caller. Where a helper answers None, pandas reports a warning at stack level 0 and a log record names "(unknown file)". The `open` event carries no `dir_fd`, so an `os.open` write passes only with an absolute path, and the calls that raise no audit event at all (`os.mkfifo`, `os.mknod`, `_posixsubprocess.fork_exec`) are replaced by a refusal before either mode's hook is armed. Since 2026-10-28 arming a guard, in every mode that runs code in process, first installs a seccomp filter (`install_exec_deny`) under which the kernel refuses every `execve` and `execveat` of the process, of every thread already running (`SECCOMP_FILTER_FLAG_TSYNC`) and of every child, so native code the hook cannot see (lets-plot's ImageMagick delegates, libc's `system`) starts no program, whatever is on `PATH`; `fork` and threads stay allowed, a filter cannot be removed, and another architecture's or an x32 syscall is refused as well. `on` needs an `execv` of a path that cannot exist to change from ENOENT before the filter to EPERM after it, so neither a sandbox policy that already refuses `execve` nor a filter the kernel accepted but does not enforce passes for it. The end line reports it (`exec_deny`: `on`, `unavailable`, `unsupported`, or `off` when the run failed before the guard was armed), and the renderer logs it with every launch (`exec_deny` in the `sandbox` event). Every in-process run requires it since the first renderer revision with the filter logged `exec_deny: on` on 2026-10-28, which shows that Cloud Run's `sandbox do` admits it (lets-plot's row alone required it before): `executor.sandbox_env` and the local backend put `ANYPLOT_DENY_EXEC=required` into every render, data run and conversion while `RENDERER_EXEC_DENY` is `required`, its default; `optional` is the switch for a platform that stops admitting the filter, under which a run goes on and reports it. A filter that is not in place stops the run before its code starts: a render with exit code 3 (which Cloud Run's launcher reports as -13, like every non-zero exit) and a stderr line that names it, a data run with `DATA_EXIT["exec_deny"]`, which the data tool reports as no fault of the code, and a conversion with `CONVERT_EXIT["exec_deny"]`, which the agents service answers `503 convert_unavailable`; both reach the host through `status.json`. Docker's default seccomp profile lets the process add it, and `ci-image.yml` checks the routes in the built image (a running thread, libc's `system`, `subprocess`, `fexecve`, a forked child). Imports, reads and `compile` stay allowed: the plot imports its libraries lazily, matplotlib reads fonts and style sheets and compiles colour and cycler strings while it draws, and a compiled code object runs only through the refused routes. A refusal raises `PermissionError`, so library code that tolerates a read-only filesystem or a missing tool (matplotlib's cache writes, `ctypes.util.find_library`, loky's `lscpu`) carries on; the harness runs scikit-learn's two process lookups (libc for threadpoolctl, the physical cores for loky) before arming when the plot names sklearn, because refused they fail the render or oversubscribe the CPU past `RLIMIT_CPU`, and since 2026-10-26 it imports cairosvg before arming when the plot names pygal or cairosvg (`warm_cairo`), because cairocffi loads libcairo at import time through the same kind of lookups (`ctypes.util.find_library`, which runs `ldconfig`). A sweep of the 650 catalogue matplotlib and seaborn files on a developer machine (Python 3.13.12, the locked plotting stack) rendered all 474 that pass the SECURITY profile under the guard; the seven it newly failed fetch over the network, call `os.chdir` or `load_dataset`, which the validator refuses anyway. With the frame refusals, a sweep of the 572 files the readiness scan admits today (clean or coupled: 285 matplotlib, 287 seaborn) rendered 565 under the guard before and after the change, with no refusal logged; the seven failures are the plots' own (each divides by the length of a title the normaliser emptied). `tests/unit/agents/runtime/test_harness_guard.py` renders nine normalised catalogue files under the guard (scikit-learn, a statsmodels graphic, mathtext, a seaborn `KMeans` file, since 2026-10-25 the `scatter-basic` files of plotnine and altair, and since 2026-10-26 pygal's `scatter-basic` and `line-basic`, whose 4800x2700 chart the canvas rewrite saves at 3200x1800, and lets-plot's `scatter-basic`; the last six are skipped on a machine without their library, while CI's test job installs every `lib-*` extra and fails such a case (`ANYPLOT_REQUIRE_LIBRARIES=1`, `tests/libraries.py`, since 2026-11-02); the renderer-image job of `ci-image.yml` renders a seaborn, a plotnine, an altair, a pygal and a lets-plot plot through the harness in the image, the altair one without `RLIMIT_AS` and the lets-plot one with its row's environment and 2048 MiB). The sweep has not yet been repeated in the renderer image. On a developer machine the spikes of 2026-10-26 rendered 273 of the 325 catalogue pygal files under the guard (the failures are the files' own off-target writes, `os.chdir` calls, missing modules and guard shapes, which the normaliser strips or the validator refuses, and one `RLIMIT_CPU` kill, waveform-audio) and 273 of the 284 eligible lets-plot files (10 divide by the length of the emptied title, and calibration-curve does not render with lets-plot 4.11.0 at all); plotnine and altair have no sweep yet. `POST /render/{job_id}/cancel` stops that job's render or data run; `GET /status` reports the version, the slot, stuck launchers, `MemAvailable`, whether the launcher exists and whether the run volume is in place. `agents/renderer/wire.py` is the JSON contract that both the service and the `remote` backend read. The renderer only runs code and returns its outputs; the host gates and the probe gates below stay in the agents service, and R1 turns a reason into a line that names the limit and the measured value, so the repair knows what to change. Every route needs an IAM-forwarded ID token whose `aud` is in `RENDERER_AUDIENCES` (the service URL, which the deploy adds, plus the OAuth client ids of the owner's user tokens) and whose `email` is in `RENDERER_ALLOWED_CALLERS`; the check is the one of `/v1`, reads `X-Serverless-Authorization` whenever it is present, and is skipped only in development. An unexpected error answers `500 {"detail": "internal"}` and is logged by its class only, so the `remote` backend never mistakes it for a front-end error and runs the job again.

The sandbox command per theme, started with `asyncio.create_subprocess_exec` in a session of its own and never a shell, with a fresh name and a fresh run directory for every launch:

```
/usr/local/gcp/bin/sandbox do --sandbox-name r-<random>-<theme> \
  --mount type=bind,source=/tmp/runs/r-<random>-<theme>/work,destination=/work -w /work \
  --env PATH=/app/.venv/bin:/usr/local/bin:/usr/bin:/bin --env HOME=/tmp --env ANYPLOT_THEME=<theme> \
  --env MPLBACKEND=Agg --env MPLCONFIGDIR=/tmp/mpl --env ANYPLOT_MPL_SEED=/opt/mplconfig \
  --env ANYPLOT_RLIMIT_CPU_S=60 --env ANYPLOT_RLIMIT_FSIZE_MB=50 --env ANYPLOT_RLIMIT_AS_MB=1024 --env ANYPLOT_RLIMIT_NPROC=64 ... \
  -- /app/.venv/bin/python -I -X disable-remote-debug /opt/anyplot/harness.py plot.py
```

What spikes S and S2 measured in europe-west4, and what the executor (`agents/renderer/executor.py`) does about it:

| Fact | Consequence |
|---|---|
| The sandbox is gVisor with no egress, no metadata server and only `lo`; `--write` makes its root filesystem writable, and with it a nested egress attempt got one step further | No `--write` and no `--allow-egress`, asserted in code; renders work without `--write` |
| A sandbox's environment holds only `HOME`, `LC_CTYPE` and the `--env` values, with no `PATH` | `PATH` is passed explicitly, with the rest of a complete environment |
| The code runs as uid 0; the image and every host file outside `/tmp` are readable from inside, sibling run directories are not, and each sandbox gets a private `/tmp` tmpfs of about 1.9 GiB | Every run directory and every user byte lives under `/tmp/runs/<run>`; the image holds no secret; the harness copies the baked font cache from `/opt/mplconfig` to `/tmp/mpl`; the fallback fonts under `/usr/share/fonts/noto` are read from the image |
| A disk fill through `/work` on the plain filesystem crashed the whole instance at about 3.4 GB | `/tmp/runs` is a 512 MiB in-memory volume, which the service checks at startup on Cloud Run (`503 volume_missing` without it), and a host watchdog measures each live run directory every 50 ms and kills the sandbox above 64 MiB (`disk_budget`) or 10,000 entries (`file_budget`); a tree deeper than 32 levels or past `PATH_MAX`, which a walk by path cannot measure, counts as over its limit |
| Each sandbox's private `/tmp` counts as instance memory, and no flag bounds it | The same watchdog reads `MemAvailable` and kills the sandbox below 512 MiB (`memory`); the remedy for the budget itself is an open decision |
| `RLIMIT_CPU`, `RLIMIT_FSIZE`, `RLIMIT_AS` and `RLIMIT_NPROC` are enforced inside; the defaults bound nothing | The harness sets 60 s, 50 MiB, 1 GiB and 64, soft and hard alike; the host keeps its wall-clock timeout |
| `sandbox delete --force` exits 0 even when it did nothing | On a timeout, the watchdog, a cancellation or an unexpected error: `os.killpg` with SIGKILL, then `sandbox delete --force <name>`; the kill counts only when the launcher process exits, and one that does not exit within 10 s is `stuck`, which refuses further renders with `503 stuck` and, on Cloud Run, ends the service's process so a fresh instance replaces it. A launcher that exits non-zero on its own also gets a `delete` |
| One of 26 renders ended in 0.4 s with `failed to exec in container ... exit status 137` and no output | A non-zero exit without the harness's `HARNESS {"event": "start"}` line is retried once with a new sandbox name; a second one is reported as `launcher`; a failure after the harness started goes to the repair |
